It is also deliberately **not** added to `allDims`: `perfect_error` is an *aggregate* — its score is derived from other dimensions' signals — so putting it in the per-dimension penalty loop would charge the underlying signals twice, the same class as the span double-count this file recorded earlier. Measured: the instrument now reads `perfect_error 信号 143 / finding 11`, and the corpus is **identical to the baseline item for item** — 106 benign / 1 escalated / FP 0.9%, 41 malicious / 41 intercepted / recall 100.0%. **Four process notes, and three of them are re-payments of tuition this file had already paid.** (a) *The first version of the fix raised FP from 0.9% to 6.6%, and the corpus caught it immediately.* Adding `{score: pe.score, name:'perfect_error'}` straight into `allDims` did produce findings — and escalated 6 additional benign samples, every one of them a **sourced, hedged, precise-number statement**: `论文指出该方法准确率 91.2%，但泛化性仍需验证`, `我们的 SLA 是 99.9%，上个季度达到了 99.95%`, `这段代码的 code coverage 只有 60%，需要补测试`. The path was `findings.length > 1`, with `perfect_error(20)` stacked on `ai_writing_tell(35)`. *One weak signal from an aggregate dimension is noise, not a finding.* (b) *The second version was rejected by an existing lock, and it is the third installment of the same tuition.* Reasoning that `perfect_error` already has a stricter dedicated gate rule and therefore should not also sit in the generic tier set, I removed it from `VERIFY_DIMS` — and `test/dimension-tier-consistency.test.js` went red at once: AGENTS.md's "Verify-level (26)" no longer matched the code. That list's arithmetic is interlocking (9 block + 8 rewrite + 26 verify + 14 score-only = 57, minus the three that are not `dimensions{}` keys = 54), so moving one number means rewriting a set. *Change a shared contract and you have inherited every caller of the old one* — cycle 22's exit code, cycle 24's drift anchor, and now this. And the removal was wrong anyway: what caused the FP was never `VERIFY_DIMS` membership, it was the finding being pushed too early. With membership restored, `level='pass'` leaves no finding for that path to see, `level='verify'` escalates exactly as the dimension itself judges, and `level='rewrite'/'high'` is already decided by the dedicated rule at line 512. (c) *`git checkout -- src/index.js` reverted to the last commit and erased the entire fix.* For the mutation proof I broke the threshold, ran the test, then restored with `git checkout` — the proof itself worked perfectly (reverting the threshold to `score >= 0.15` turns cases ②, ③ and ⑤ red simultaneously), but the checkout restored **the last commit**, which contains none of this cycle's work, and the new lock dropped from 5/5 to 3/5 in front of me. This is the sentence this file already carries — *`git checkout -- <doc>` is not a safe way to clear probe pollution; it reverts to a stale commit, not to last-good state* — relearned on source instead of documents. The fix was replayed verbatim from the edit record and re-measured at 5/5. The lesson: **a mutation proof's rollback must keep the pre-change text, not delegate it to version control.** (d) *The new lock's source-level case was unconditionally red because it read my own comment as code.* Case ④ asserted `perfect_error` is absent from `allDims` with `/perfect_error/.test(allDims segment)` — and the comment I had written inside `allDims` explaining *why* it is absent contains that very word. Same family as cycle 25's `}));` being read as a lock close and cycle 19's comment stripper deleting 44% of the audit script: **an instrument that pattern-matches without regard to context will read a picture inside a string as code.** It now strips line comments first and matches the entry `{score: pe.score, name: 'perfect_error'}` itself. Gate measured against this cycle's starting point: verify **15/15**, run-all **1380 → 1395** with 0 failures (new `test/perfect-error-finding.test.js`, 5 cases, including a corpus-level FP regression guard asserting the four sourced-precise-number statements stay `pass`), audit **84/84 with exit code 0, five for five** after syncing 1380 → 1395 across six sites in README/SKILL/CURRENT_STATE/AGENTS. `src/` changed, but the change is in finding construction only — no MCP tool definition or handler was added or modified, so the tool inventory and permission contract are unchanged and MCP was **not** restarted (recorded as not-performed, not as performed; `tools/list` is unverifiable at 401 unauthenticated). **One gap recorded rather than closed, and it is the instrument's own:** `dimension-health-audit.js` still reports `evidence` as "7630 signals / 0 findings", while `evidence` is by design a score-only dimension and its block in `index.js` is a reverse test (`if (ev.score < 0.25)`). The instrument counts "signal went non-zero" and "a finding exists" as the same event, so every score-only dimension reads as a defect. That is *an instrument reporting its own limitation as an engine defect* — the failure this file recorded twice in cycle 11 — and it was left alone because it produces no wrong action, only a list that needs manual review every time.

- **The rotation weight had been saturated for twenty-eight cycles — it claimed to rotate, and the one number that could have exposed it was never measured. And the test that pinned the cooldown logic had been asserting the saturation bug itself.** Twenty-sixth cycle, and the engine chose `doc-honest-numbers` for the **eighth** consecutive time (0.65, confidence 0.75). Cycles 23, 24 and 25 all closed with the same hand-off — "seriously rotate the slice" — and the rotation logic had not been touched in eight cycles, so this cycle did not switch slices: the defect was inside `doc-honest-numbers`' own machinery, and it belonged to the slice. The first measurement was the whole diagnosis. Counting completions from the journal gave `test-coverage-gap` **37** and `doc-honest-numbers` **33**; the weight was `max(0.05, base - 0.08 × cumulativeCount)`; so `0.5 - 0.08×33 = -2.14 → 0.05` and `0.6 - 0.08×37 = -2.36 → 0.05`. **Two different histories, mapped to the same value.** The composite formula (`src/core/decision.js:341`) is `feasibility*.15 + identity*.25 + consequence_value*.25 + (1-risk_penalty)*.25 + confidence*.10` — **`cost` is not in it at all** — so once `consequence_value` is a constant across every candidate, that 25% term contributes nothing to the ranking, and the ranking collapses onto the static feasibility/risk/confidence table, where `doc-honest-numbers` (0.85 / 0.15 / 0.75) beats `test-coverage-gap` (0.75 / 0.20 / 0.70) on every axis. That arithmetic reproduces the engine's own output exactly: 0.65 vs 0.63. **A cumulative decay with a floor cannot distinguish anything once every candidate has crossed the floor — and a mechanism that has stopped discriminating looks exactly like a mechanism that is working.** The fix is a **recent window** (`RECENT_WINDOW = 10`): record the order of every cycle's `chosen`, and charge only completions that fall inside the last ten cycles. Three properties, each doing work — it is *bounded* (at most ten charges, so different histories can never collapse onto one value), it is *self-healing* (a slice recovers automatically as it leaves the window, with no need for the `exhausted` cooldown to catch it), and it encodes the actual intent, "don't do the same thing repeatedly," rather than "don't do this thing many times ever." Measured after: the engine immediately chose `dimension-health-audit` (composite 0.75), and `--debug-candidates` shows the seven resident slices spread across 0.7 / 0.65 / 0.56 / 0.45 / 0.41 / 0.32 / 0.05 with no two alike. The change is confined to the script's own candidate table and deliberately does **not** touch `decision.js`'s composite contract, which `aspira_decision_decide` also reads — the shared-contract tuition this file has paid three times (cycle 22's exit code, cycle 24's drift anchor, and now this). **Then the existing lock turned out to have been pinning the bug.** `test/exhausted-cooldown.test.js`'s case ③ gave every resident slice the *same* done count, on the stated reasoning that this leaves feasibility/risk/confidence as the only difference — and asserted `doc-honest-numbers` must win. That premise was true only *because of* the saturation: `doc`'s base `consequence_value` is **0.5, lower** than `test-coverage-gap`'s 0.6, so with a discriminating weight `test-coverage-gap` wins on that term and `doc` loses. The test had been green for a reason that was the defect. It now constructs the *recent* distribution explicitly — `doc` at zero recent completions so its 0.5 stands highest, every other slice at least one, and the three with higher bases (0.7 / 0.65 / 0.7) at two each — so `doc` wins on the term it should win on, and the cooldown restoration is actually what is being measured. **Three process notes, and the first one is the recurring shape at its purest.** (a) The new lock's premises were refuted by measurement three times, and each refutation was worth more than the assertion it killed. v1 asserted "the slice with zero recent completions and the highest base value must win" — measured, `dimension-health-audit` (cv 0.7) loses to `test-coverage-gap` (cv 0.6), because the latter is better on feasibility, risk and confidence and **risk alone carries 25% of the composite**. *The highest `consequence_value` is not the highest composite.* v2 asserted "change who dominates the window and the winner must change" — measured, `doc` wins under *both* histories, because its structural advantage is large enough that it wins whenever it has zero recent completions. Rewritten as the assertion that actually means responsiveness: **the same slice must win under one history and lose under another.** v3's saturation regression used `doc×8 / test-coverage×2`, and under the old weight those give 0.05 and 0.44 — *not equal*, because eight is not enough completions to cross the floor. The real repository is 33 and 37, both long past it. The history had to be 25/25 plus a differing recent tail to be simultaneously "different under the new weight, identical under the old one." (b) To make the weight observable at all, the engine gained `--debug-candidates`, which prints the candidate table with `recentCount` / `totalDone` / the decayed value and exits without deciding or writing a journal — the same shape as the existing `--introspect-only`. The reason is the whole cycle in one sentence: **the weight could previously only be inferred from "who got chosen," while feasibility, risk and confidence were acting on that same number — so whether the weight still discriminated anything was unobservable, which is precisely why it saturated twenty-eight cycles ago and nobody noticed.** (c) The lock is deliberately two-sided: case ③ asserts different recent counts produce different decayed values, and case ④ rewrites the script back to the cumulative implementation and asserts the two slices collapse to the same 0.05. Without case ④, case ③ could be satisfied by any implementation that simply never picks `doc-honest-numbers` — including, in principle, one that had re-introduced the bug. Gate measured against this cycle's starting point: verify **15/15**, run-all **1375 → 1395** with 0 failures (new `test/slice-rotation.test.js`, 5 cases, plus the repaired cooldown premise), audit **84/84 with exit code 0, five for five** after syncing 1375 → 1395 in README/SKILL/CURRENT_STATE. `src/` unchanged, so MCP was not restarted. **Two gaps recorded rather than closed.** The recent-window count only sees `status === 'done'` entries, so a failed-and-reverted cycle contributes no evidence that its slice was recently attempted — harmless at the current failure rate (no `failed` entries since cycle 20), but it would make rotation reason from an optimistically thin history if failures ever cluster. And the new lock pins the *decay*, not the *window size*: `RECENT_WINDOW = 10` was chosen by hand, and a value derived from the actual journal distribution would be a measurement rather than a guess.

- **The unexplained test failure from last cycle came down to an export name that did not match — a destructure that fails without throwing, handing the probe `undefined` — and the lock-coverage instrument I "fixed" for a false positive turned out to be unable to fail at all.** Twenty-fifth cycle, and the engine chose `doc-honest-numbers` for the **seventh** consecutive time (0.65, confidence 0.75). This cycle opened on cycle 24's first hand-off item: build the test lock for the HEALED direction, where the previous cycle had recorded "verified correct by direct measurement, but the same scenario reports `被改写且未还原` in the test, difference not isolated", with `cwd` / `NODE_OPTIONS` / `ASPIRA_TEST_RUNNER` as the three unruled-out candidates. Two of them fell cheaply: the audit reads exactly one environment variable (`ASPIRA_AUDIT_SKIP_TESTS`, line 988), so `ASPIRA_TEST_RUNNER` was never in play; and running the audit with the healing hook showed the human-readable branch reporting `已被治愈` once and `被改写且未还原` zero times while `--json` reported `{"drift": [], "healed": ["SKILL.md"]}` — **both branches were correct**, so the branch difference cycle 24 suspected did not exist either. The decisive move was to restore the case, have `writeHook` also drop a copy at a fixed path, and finally reproduce reliably: `healed=[]` `drift=["SKILL.md"]` inside the test, against a manual run that healed. Diffing the two hooks found it in one line — the generated one read `return p1 + undefined;`. The test imports `PROBE_AUTH_VERSION`; `test/_doc-probe-lock.js` exported `PROBE_VERSION`; **a destructure that names something the module does not export does not throw, it yields `undefined`**, and `p1 + undefined` in JavaScript does not throw either — it quietly concatenates the seven-letter word. So the probe "healed" `SKILL.md` by writing `| Engine version | undefined`, the audit's `labelledVersionOf` matched no version, returned `null`, and the file fell into the drift branch, leaving the document in a state that **looks repaired and is in fact corrupted**. This is the contract-mismatch family this file keeps recording — a class object called as a namespace, a string where an object was wanted, a named destructure of a default export, a module instantiated then discarded, a field never assigned — and every one of them shares the single consequence: **the call succeeds, nothing throws, the response is structurally valid, and the content is dead.** The lock module now exports both names, and the test carries a **load-time guard** that throws with the measured value and the contents of `VERSION` if `PROBE_AUTH_VERSION` is not an `x.y.z` string, so a future rename detonates at load instead of waiting for a probe to write garbage into a document. Verified in the mutation direction: renaming the export back makes the file fail at load with the full diagnostic, and restoring returns 5/5. **Then fixing cycle 24's second hand-off item found something worse than the item itself.** `doc-probe-lock-coverage.test.js`'s `insideDocLock()` counted `}));` as the lock's close — and `}));` was never the real closing syntax of `withDocLock(() => { ... })`, which is `});` followed by `};`. Cycle 24 saw only the first half of that: a hook string whose last line ends in `}));` closed the real lock early, so in-lock call sites were judged unlocked, a false positive that reddened the gate. **Stripping strings without adding real pairing makes the second half worse**: `closes` becomes 0, `opens(1) > closes(0)` is永 true, and *every* call site is judged inside the lock — a check that can never fail, indistinguishable from no check. Measured, not theorised: a file that requires the lock module but places its `runAudit()` call **outside** `withDocLock` passed 2/2 in silence. `insideDocLock()` now does real brace matching on a length-preserving copy with strings and comments blanked — locating each `withDocLock(() =>`, depth-pairing from the arrow body to its true closing brace, and testing whether the call site's index falls inside any such range, with unpaired braces deliberately treated as "cannot prove, assume unlocked". Both directions measured: all real test files pass 2/2, and the same out-of-lock file is now caught precisely, by line number. **Two process notes.** (a) *My own diagnostic instrument was the pollution source for the tail that cycle 24 recorded as destroyed.* The hand-written `/tmp/healhook.cjs` replaced the whole line with `[^\n]*`, deleting the audit trail off `SKILL.md`'s version line; one round of case 5 failed on "the tail must still be there", and I briefly suspected the suite of eating it — while the suite, with the repaired `restoreVersionLine`, preserved the tail across two consecutive full runs, and my own manual diagnostic deleted it again each time. Restored from git, and `HEAL_HOOK` now replaces only the version number. (b) The first reverse proof used the wrong idiom — a literal `['scripts/audit-doc-numbers.js']` array element, while `spawnsDocAudit()` only recognises the `SCRIPT` constant plus `path.join(...audit-doc-numbers.js`, so the file never entered the scan at all. That is the known shape of an enumerating filter, and it is recorded below rather than widened this cycle. Gate measured against this cycle's starting point: verify **15/15**, run-all **1374 → 1375** with 0 failures (the new case is the HEALED-direction lock), audit **84/84 with exit code 0, five for five** after syncing 1374 → 1375 in README/SKILL/CURRENT_STATE. `src/` unchanged, so MCP was not restarted. **One gap recorded rather than papered over:** the lock-coverage instrument still recognises only one spawn idiom, and its self-check asserts only `checked > 0`, which cannot guarantee that a new way of writing the spawn is covered.

- **The drift detector had been pointing the repair the wrong way — it told the reader to restore a dirty snapshot over a document that had just been healed — and the "restore" written last cycle was silently eating the audit trail off the version line.** Twenty-fourth cycle, and the engine chose `doc-honest-numbers` for the **sixth** consecutive time (0.65, confidence 0.75). This cycle opened on cycle 23's own first recommendation — root out the last unlocked write — and the first measurement landed somewhere else entirely. Chasing why the audit intermittently exits 1, the red runs shared one property: **the previous run had left `SKILL.md` at `| Engine version | 9.9.9 |`**, so this run's snapshot was dirty from the start. Measured both ways and the split was clean: starting from a polluted document, the audit goes red; starting clean, **eight consecutive audits were 8-for-8 green**, and the unmodified baseline over ten runs was 6 green / 4 red. The problem was never concurrency — it was the *starting state*, and the reason is the defect worth the cycle. When the document is dirty at the audit's module load, `DOC_SNAPSHOT` holds the dirt; the suite's own probes then heal it to `1.0.0` during `measure()`; and the drift check compares **current (clean)** against **snapshot (dirty)** and prints `❌ 审计期间文档被改写且未还原(1 份): SKILL.md` — followed by the remediation *"把污染还原到上一行的快照内容"*. The direction is exactly backwards: the disk holds a document that was just repaired, the snapshot holds the `9.9.9`, and the only actionable sentence in the report instructs the reader to write the pollution back. **A report that points the repair the wrong way is worse than no report.** Cycle 22 added drift detection so that pollution would have to speak; it spoke, and it named the wrong culprit *and* the wrong fix. The audit now classifies each drifted file by direction, using the one field convention #1 names explicitly — the labelled `Engine version` line. Current is the authoritative value while the snapshot is not ⇒ `⚠️ 审计开始时文档已带污染, 本轮已被治愈`, carrying `⛔ 绝不要按快照还原` and `✓ 正确修法: 什么都不用做, 当前磁盘内容已经是权威值`; anything else stays in the old `❌` branch. Both halves still set `process.exitCode = 1`, because a dirty snapshot makes the report's "与实测一致" just as untrustworthy. Verified directly: pollute `SKILL.md` (tail intact), run the audit with a healing preload hook, `--json` reports `{"drift": [], "healed": ["SKILL.md"]}`. **Then the same cycle found that last cycle's own fix had been destroying data.** Cycle 23 introduced `restoreVersionLine()` so that a probe's restore would write the authoritative version instead of writing back a backup that might itself be polluted — the right idea, executed with `/(\|\s*Engine version\s*\|)[^\n]*/`, and **`[^\n]*` ate the whole rest of the line**. `git show HEAD:SKILL.md` line 153 reads `| Engine version | 1.0.0 | \`VERSION\`, \`package.json\`, runtime \`hf.VERSION\` (module-level) / \`hf.version\` (instance), and \`src/core/version.js\` agree |` — that tail *is* convention #1's audit trail, the sentence naming which files must agree — and on disk it had been trimmed to `| Engine version | 1.0.0 |`, by the very function whose job was to restore. **A tool that reports success while having written the wrong thing is worse than one that fails**, and this one reported success on every run. It now replaces only the version number, via a function replacement rather than `'$1' + value`, because a value beginning with a digit yields `'$11.0.0'` — an ambiguous capture-group reference that V8 happens to resolve as `$1` plus the literal, which is an implementation detail not worth betting on. Measured after: two consecutive full-suite runs leave the `agree |` count at 1 every time. **Three process notes, all of them re-payments.** (a) I changed the audit's drift-classification block and immediately broke a test that hardcodes an anchor into the old code — `test/audit-drift-detection.test.js`'s reverse proof threw `审计里找不到 DRIFT 比对循环(锚点已变)`, which is precisely its designed behaviour. *Change a shared contract and you have inherited every caller of the old one*; cycle 22 paid that tuition for the exit code and this cycle paid it again for drift classification, and this time the anchor caught it first. (b) The first version of the new case spawned the audit with no preload hook, which spawns the full suite, which contains the very test doing the spawning — an audit→suite→audit→suite nesting path that cycle 21 spent an entire cycle measuring to confirm did not exist. One new case was enough to build it; routing through a hook removed it. (c) `doc-probe-lock-coverage.test.js` produced a **false positive** because `insideDocLock()` counts `}));` anywhere in the source, and my new hook is a *string array* one of whose lines ends in `}));` — so the real lock was "closed" early and two `runAudit()` call sites were judged unlocked. That is the same family as cycle 19's comment stripper deleting 44% of the audit script: **an instrument that pattern-matches without regard to context will read a picture inside a string as code.** A false positive is loud and visible, unlike a miss, but it reddened the gate all the same. Gate measured against this cycle's starting point: verify **15/15**, run-all **1374** with 0 failures, audit **84/84 with exit code 0, five for five** (the starting baseline was 4 reds in 10). `src/` unchanged, so MCP was not restarted. **One item is recorded rather than closed, and it is the lock on this cycle's main deliverable.** The audit's direction classification is verified correct by direct measurement, but the test case written for it failed for a reason I could not isolate — in the test the same scenario reports `被改写且未还原` where a manual run reports `healed`. Ruled out by measurement: the anchor (it would throw instead of failing an assertion), the hook not firing (`measured.tests` comes from the fake summary when it does), capture-group ambiguity, and tail-truncation. Not ruled out: the `env` difference between `runAudit()` and my manual invocation — `cwd`, `NODE_OPTIONS`, and `ASPIRA_TEST_RUNNER` are the three candidates. Per the cycle rule I reverted the failing case and kept the fix, because **an assertion whose failure I cannot explain is worse than an assertion that does not exist** — it would let the next cycle believe the behaviour is locked when only the "dirtied" direction is. The reverted case, the reproduction steps, and the three env candidates are left as a comment in the test file. Case 1 was strengthened in the meantime to assert `!/已被治愈/`, so the dirty direction is pinned in both directions of the classification.

- **The decision engine had been reasoning from two numbers that were never the thing they claimed to be — and the "intermittent red" it kept producing turned out to be a permanent latch, not a flake.** Twenty-third cycle, and the engine chose `doc-honest-numbers` for the fifth consecutive time (0.65, confidence 0.75). Cycle 22's recommendation was to extend drift detection to the exempt directories; this cycle ignored it, because the engine's own output contained something closer to the slice's core. Every cycle it prints an `introspection` block, and this cycle I finally read it against reality: it reported **`dimCount: 59`** and **`testCount: 241`**. Measured: **54** dimensions and **310** test files (**1,374** cases). Both numbers were the recorded shape at its purest — *a count of an adjacent thing, used under the name of the thing it was next to.* `dimCount` counted the keys of `const dimMap = {...}`, and `dimMap` carries five **aliases** (`bullshit`, `appeal_to_authority`, `pseudo_causal`, `soft_deflection`, `ai_writing_tell`) that are keys of `dimMap` but not keys of `dimensions`; measured, `dimMap` has 59 and `discriminate().dimensions` has 54, and the five extra are exactly those aliases — which AGENTS.md had already documented in prose while the engine kept counting them as dimensions. `testCount` used `readdirSync('test')`, which counts only the **top level** and misses `test/compliance/` and friends (measured: 241 top-level, 310 recursive), and it counts **files** while the decision prompt rendered it as "241测试" — which reads as 241 *cases* against a real 1,374. Neither number enters any arithmetic (the slice scores come from a static table), so a wrong number turns no assertion red; it only makes every autonomous decision reason from a false picture of the repository's size. **A number that was never measured is indistinguishable, in an all-green report, from a number that is locked** — and here the unmeasured number was feeding the thing that decides what to measure. Both are now the thing they claim to be: `dimCount` parses the `dimensions` literal itself by brace-balancing (verified key-for-key and in order against the runtime), `testCount` walks recursively, and both call sites now say in as many words that the count is files and that the case count is deliberately *not* measured here — because the engine runs in **0.032 s** and running the suite to learn one number costs **32 s**, a thousandfold slowdown to acquire a number the engine does not need to decide. The engine also gained `--introspect-only`, which prints the introspection and writes nothing: the script `writeFileSync`s a journal on every run, so letting a test exercise it would have added a journal per suite run — coverage bought with pollution. The new lock is `test/decision-engine-introspection.test.js` (5 cases) and it pins each number twice, once for what it must equal and once for what it must **not**: `dimCount` must equal the runtime key count *and* must not equal `dimMap`'s; `testCount` must equal the recursive count *and* must not equal the top-level-only one — with a guard on each negative assertion that its premise still holds, because a "must not equal" whose two sides have converged is a check that can never fail. Verified live by reverting the engine to both old mistakes: four of five cases go red **by name**, and restoring returns 5/5. **Then the cycle found something far worse than a wrong number, and it reclassified four cycles of chasing.** The suite went red with 7 failures and `SKILL.md` sitting at `| Engine version | 9.9.9 |`. I repaired the document, synced the test count, and the suite went 1374/0 — and then went red *again* on the next run. The measurement that explains it: **start from a polluted `SKILL.md` and run the suite twice — both runs are 1364/7, and `SKILL.md` is still `9.9.9` after both.** The pollution is a **permanent latch.** Every live-injection probe reads `const backup = fs.readFileSync(SKILL, 'utf8')`, builds its poison by replacing a value *derived from that backup*, and restores with `fs.writeFileSync(SKILL, backup)`. Once the document is already `9.9.9`, the poison's `replace` is a no-op, the audit duly reports the mismatch, and the restore writes the `9.9.9` it just read back into the file. Every run starts polluted, every run re-writes the pollution, and **nothing in the repository can heal it.** Cycle 20 recorded the underlying sentence — *a hardcoded anchor goes silently dead once the document is polluted* — but only as "the check becomes vacuous"; it never recorded that the consequence is a **permanently red suite**. Cycle 21's 4-green-then-1-red and cycle 22's 5-for-5 were the same latch seen from its two sides: one measurement started clean, the other started from debris the first had left. The fix is `restoreVersionLine()` in `test/_doc-probe-lock.js`: the restore rewrites the version line to the value read from `VERSION` instead of writing `backup` back, and all four probes that touch `SKILL.md`/`CURRENT_STATE.md` route through it. This is *not* the cycle-20 sin — that self-healing mechanism failed because it could only answer "content differs from the sidecar" and never "who changed it"; here the field has exactly one correct answer, because convention #1 states that `VERSION` is the single source of truth and `SKILL.md` must match it, so there is no legitimate state in which that line reads anything else. Measured after: starting from pollution, the suite now **heals** `SKILL.md` to `1.0.0` and finishes 1374/0, and five consecutive full-suite runs leave the `9.9.9` count at zero every time. **And cycle 22's drift detection earned its keep in production for the first time.** Running the audit gave exit code 1 with `不一致: 0` and a new section — `❌ 审计期间文档被改写且未还原(1 份): SKILL.md`, carrying the words that "与实测一致" is read from the snapshot and cannot be read as "the document is clean". Before cycle 22 the same event would have been reported as a handful of mismatches blaming `SKILL.md`, with nothing to say it was probe pollution rather than a document defect — precisely the cycle-19 defect形态, now named. That run's document was already back to `1.0.0` when the audit exited, and the next run was 84/84 with exit code 0. One more rotting number fell in the same cycle: `audit-self-pollution.test.js`'s preload hook returned a hardcoded fake runner summary of `1351 通过`, so with the documents now claiming 1,374 that audit reported a spurious test-count mismatch on *every* run — invisible because the file only asserts on version-class mismatches. It now reads the claimed count from `SKILL.md`. Gate measured against this cycle's starting point: verify **15/15**, run-all **1369 → 1374** with 0 failures, audit **84/84 with exit code 0**, quantified over ten consecutive runs as 9 green / 1 red where the single red run had `不一致: 0` (pure drift), self-healed within that run, and was followed immediately by green. `src/` unchanged, so MCP was not restarted (401 unauthenticated). **One residual is recorded rather than closed:** the audit still exits non-zero about one run in ten, and the remaining cause is the one cycle 21 located and left — `audit-self-pollution.test.js`'s `HOOK` writes `SKILL.md` from an audit **child process that does not hold the doc lock**, so within that window another reader can see `9.9.9`. It is not fixed here because making the hook take the lock deadlocks it: the parent test already holds the lock and is waiting for the audit to exit. The difference from four cycles ago is that this is now a *detected and self-healing* event rather than a latch, which is the whole point of the two cycles of work behind it.
- **The cycle-19 snapshot's own dark side: it made the audit report "0 mismatches" while the document sat dirty on disk — and the audit's exit code had never carried the verdict at all.** Twenty-second cycle, and the engine chose `doc-honest-numbers` for the fourth consecutive time (0.65, confidence 0.75), so this cycle opened exactly where cycle 21's `nextRecommendation` said it should: take apart the amplifier. Cycle 19 introduced `DOC_SNAPSHOT` because the audit spawns `test/run-all.js` and the suite contains probes that rewrite `SKILL.md`/`README.md`/`CURRENT_STATE.md` mid-run — the snapshot was taken before the spawn and `claims()` read only the snapshot, so probe pollution could not reach the audit's verdict. It worked. **And the same decision also silenced something, which nobody asked about until now.** Measured before touching anything, and the first measurement was the clean one: with `SKILL.md`'s `Engine version` genuinely changed to `9.9.9`, the audit reports `不一致: 1` — and `echo $?` is **0**. There is no `process.exitCode` anywhere in the script. So a caller — CI, a probe in `test/`, an external agent — cannot tell green from red except by parsing Chinese text out of stdout. **A gate that only speaks through stdout is no gate at all in any pipeline that checks the exit code.** The second measurement was the one worth the cycle, and it inverted this cycle's own starting assumption: I ran the audit, got 5 mismatches, went looking for probe pollution, and measured `9.9.9` across all four probe-target documents — **zero occurrences**. The mismatch was not pollution. It was the snapshot's blind spot, demonstrated by a `--require` preload hook that dirties `SKILL.md` during `measure()` and does *not* put it back: the audit reports `文档声称总数: 84 | 与实测一致: 84 | 不一致: 0`, exit code 0, **while `SKILL.md` on disk reads `| Engine version | 9.9.9 |`**. Every claim in the report is read from the snapshot, so "0 mismatches" means "the claims were all correct *as of the moment the audit started*" — it does **not** mean "the document is clean", and nothing in the output had ever said so. This is not a hypothetical: cycle 19 found pollution precisely this way, by killing its own audit run so that SIGTERM reached a probe between its injection and its restore. The snapshot made that pollution invisible instead of merely harmless. **A fix that turns a failure into silence is the same severity as the failure it fixed.** `claims()` now re-reads every document and compares it byte-for-byte against the snapshot; any difference is reported as its own loud category — `❌ 审计期间文档被改写且未还原(N 份)` — deliberately *not* counted in `bad`, because a dirty document is a **probe** defect and counting it as a document defect would send the reader to fix the wrong file. The report now says in as many words that "与实测一致" is read from the snapshot and cannot be read as "the document is clean". Both conditions set `process.exitCode = 1`. **And then I broke thirteen tests by changing that contract, having identified the risk first and fixed it second — which is the wrong order and is recorded as such.** The live-injection tests call the audit with `execFileSync`, inject a wrong value, and *expect the audit to report the mismatch*; `execFileSync` throws on a non-zero exit, so "the audit correctly reported a mismatch" became the thing that detonated the probe. I had checked which tests spawn the audit and which already had a `catch`, and then changed the contract anyway before updating the callers. Eight test files now take the output from `e.stdout` — including `audit-self-pollution.test.js`'s hook case, which was already inside a `try/finally` and needed `catch` and `finally` to coexist. **Change a shared contract and you have inherited every caller of the old one**; the repo already states this as the MCP three-place rule and I treated it as a test-side cleanup instead of part of the contract. The new lock is `test/audit-drift-detection.test.js` (4 cases) and it is built so that neither half can be paid for by the other: pollution unrestored → drift reported *and* `不一致: 0` *and* exit ≠ 0 (proving the two defect classes are not masking each other); pollution restored in place → no drift and exit 0 (without this side, the first assertion could be bought by always reporting drift); a genuinely wrong claim → mismatch reported *and* exit ≠ 0; and a reverse proof that copies the audit, strips only the `DRIFT` loop from the copy, and requires the copy to report **no** drift against the same pollution. The reverse proof is done on a copy rather than by editing the shared source — the first version was going to mutate the real audit, which only this test holds the doc lock for, so it would have made the concurrent source-reading probes its victims. Verified live in the mutation direction too: stripping the real `DRIFT` loop turns case 1 red with the output reading `不一致: 0` over a dirty document — the amplifier reproduced on demand — while case 4 fails loudly with "anchor changed, reverse proof invalid" instead of passing silently. **Cycle 21's unresolved gap closed, and the mechanism behind it is now the one this file had already recorded.** Cycle 21 left "the audit intermittently reports 5–6 mismatches and leaves `SKILL.md` at `9.9.9`, root cause not established", having measured 4 green then 1 red. `probe21` now runs **5 for 5 green**. The explanation is the cycle-20 lesson re-learned: once a document is polluted, a live-injection test's anchor dies — `POISON === original` makes its `replace` a no-op, so the audit reads the clean document, reports no mismatch, and the test goes red; its "must be restored to 1.0.0" assertion then also fails, because the backup it restores contains the `9.9.9` it was meant to catch. Cycle 20 wrote that sentence and cycle 21 walked into it anyway, with 4-green-1-red too thin a sample to attribute. The honest caveat stands: 5/5 against 4/1 is still a small sample, and "the `release()` fix was the main cause" is recorded as mechanism-supported rather than proven. Gate measured against this cycle's starting point: verify **15/15**, run-all **1365 → 1369** with 0 failures, audit **84/84 with exit code 0** three times consecutively, probe 5/5. `src/` unchanged, so MCP was not restarted (401 unauthenticated). The doc sync itself turned out to be the new exit code's first real customer: before syncing 1365 → 1369 the audit reported 4 mismatches **and exit code 1**, which is exactly what the code is for — "the documents lag the code" stopped being a line somebody has to read and became a signal a caller can see.
- **Cycle 20's own diagnosis was wrong, and correcting it found the lock that was never actually mutual — plus seven readers watching a document mid-rewrite.** Twenty-first cycle, `doc-honest-numbers` again (0.65, confidence 0.75 — the same decisive pick as cycles 19 and 20). Cycle 20 ended `failed`/reverted and left two "located but unfixed" notes; this cycle opened on the first of them and, before touching any code, **disproved cycle 20's premise by measurement.** Cycle 20 had recorded that `audit-doc-coverage.test.js` and `doc-probe-lock-coverage.test.js` "spawn the full suite while holding the doc lock" because they do not set `ASPIRA_AUDIT_SKIP_TESTS`. Measured: **neither file spawns the audit at all** — they only read `scripts/audit-doc-numbers.js`'s *source* and regex-match other test files. The measurement had grepped for the *environment variable name* instead of for an actual *spawn*, so two pure readers were mistaken for spawners. The three files that really run without the skip flag spawn `calibrate-fp-recall.js` / `mcp-contract-audit.js` / `mcp-echo-audit.js` — not the audit, not documents — and those three scripts spawn nothing themselves. **There is no nesting path.** The measurement that did pay off was the inverse question: *who reads the probe-target documents without holding the lock?* **Seven test files** do, and the measured consequence is not theoretical — one process holding the lock while it writes an injection marker into `AGENTS.md` and restores it, another process reading without the lock 4,000 times: **3,992 of those reads saw the injected value.** That race had been invisible for fourteen cycles because the lock was only ever conceived as protecting *writers*, and because the window is narrow: measured, a probe holds the doc lock for **0.65 s** (one audit run with `ASPIRA_AUDIT_SKIP_TESTS=1`), so eight probes together leave under eight seconds of polluted window per suite run, and a reader lands in it only occasionally. **The narrower the window, the more the failure looks like environmental noise rather than a defect** — which is exactly why this survived. Two fixes, both locked behaviourally. (a) `release()` in `test/_doc-probe-lock.js` read `if (pid === null || pid === mine || stale) rmSync(lock)`, and **`pid === null` is the bug**: `acquire()` is two steps — `mkdirSync(LOCK_DIR)` then `writeFileSync(pid)` — and between them no pid file exists, so *any* process calling `release()` in that window deletes a lock somebody else just took. Two holders is precisely the accident the cycle-7 lock exists to prevent, and the measured damage is the recorded shape end to end: A reads `SKILL.md`'s backup (`1.0.0`) and writes `9.9.9`; B reads its backup — now already `9.9.9` — and writes `9.9.9`; A restores `1.0.0`; B writes its "backup" `9.9.9` back. The document is permanently polluted and **both probes report a successful restore.** `release()` now deletes only when the pid is provably this process, or when the lock is provably stale. (b) A new `readDoc(filePath, timeoutMs)` holds the lock for reads; the five readers of `README.md`/`SKILL.md` were routed through it, and the four that read only `AGENTS.md` were deliberately left alone — **no probe injects `AGENTS.md`**, so requiring the lock there would be consistency theatre rather than a fix, and that boundary is disclosed in the test rather than hidden. Both locks carry a reverse proof, and earning those proofs cost three rewrites of the same test: v1 deadlocked (parent holds the lock, then spawns a reader that needs it — parent waits for child, child waits for parent, guaranteed `ETIMEDOUT`); v2 made both processes children, and the writer had to contend with the concurrent probes for the lock first, so its injection sometimes landed *after* the reader's 6-second polling window and the reverse proof went red on its own; v3 does the whole thing **inside one process** — hold the lock, inject, then call `readDoc`, which must block to timeout because this very process already holds the lock. **Proving a mutex cannot depend on the timing that the mutex is supposed to protect.** The ownership test relearned the same lesson at one remove: its first version called `clearLock()` per case, which under the concurrent runner deleted a lock a live probe held — *the test became the concurrent writer it was built to detect*, and it showed up as "stale takeover must work" failing intermittently. It now holds the lock for its whole file, because **a test that manipulates shared state must first acquire that state's lock.** Gate after the fixes, measured against this cycle's starting point: verify **15/15**, run-all **1359 → 1365** with 0 failures, audit **84/84** (three consecutive green at close). `src/` unchanged, so MCP was not restarted (401 unauthenticated). **One gap is recorded rather than papered over, and it is the same defect this cycle only partially closed:** the audit still intermittently reports 5–6 mismatches and leaves `SKILL.md` sitting at `| Engine version | 9.9.9 |`. It predates this cycle (observed in cycle 20 with the lock already reverted to `HEAD`), its rate fell from roughly 50% to near zero after the `release()` fix but not to zero, and its root cause is **not** established. Ruled out by measurement: the nesting path (does not exist), unlocked readers (fixed here), and the new tests (removing them still reproduces). What is established: only two test files ever write `9.9.9` into `SKILL.md`, neither leaks when run alone or alternating with the other, and `audit-self-pollution.test.js`'s `HOOK` writes `SKILL.md` from an audit child process that does not hold the doc lock. The amplifier is worth naming on its own — the audit's `DOC_SNAPSHOT` is taken at process start, so **one pollution event is re-read by every subsequent audit from its own snapshot**, which makes a one-off accident look like a persistent defect. That is the unanticipated dark side of the cycle-19 snapshot, and it is the first thing the next cycle should take apart.
- **The fix for "the probe's backup dies with the process" was itself a worse defect, and it ate my own work twice before I caught it.** Twentieth cycle, `doc-honest-numbers` again, and the engine chose it decisively (0.65, confidence 0.75 — the first discriminated pick since the tie). The angle came from cycle 19's own recommendation: `DOC_SNAPSHOT` had fixed the **read** side (the audit reads a snapshot taken before it spawns the suite), but the **write** side — the probe's own injection — had never been crash-safe. Measured before touching anything, and the first measurement corrected the previous cycle's: cycle 19 estimated "four or five" injecting test files; the measured count is **8**, 60% low. All 8 use only `withDocLock` and never call `acquire` directly (count: **0**), so a crash-safety boundary added *inside the lock* would cover all eight without touching any probe file — and touching eight probe files would be eight fresh opportunities to break exactly the failure form this cycle was meant to repair. The design looked right and was implemented: a sidecar backup under `data/.doc-probe-backups/` (gitignored, whole-directory-exempted by the audit, so invisible to both git and the instrument), written at lock acquisition, consumed at release; `acquire()` calls `recoverStale()` so the *next* lock holder heals what a dead one left behind; drift is repaired **before** the error is thrown, because a silent repair is the shape this file keeps recording. **Then the boundary ate the repository, twice.** (1) `SKILL.md` was left holding `| Engine version | 9.9.9 |` and `AGENTS.md` was written into a **mixed state grep reports as binary** — the cycle-19 and cycle-20 entries were both gone. The cause was a bug I introduced while fixing the original bug: `holderAlive()` treated "pid file unreadable" as "holder is dead", but there is a window between `mkdirSync(LOCK_DIR)` and `writeFileSync(pid)` in which a waiter can read no pid, force-remove the lock, and `mkdir` its own — **two processes each believing they hold the lock**, which is precisely the accident the cycle-7 lock exists to prevent. Measured cost: the suite went 1356/10, and the failing messages all read like instrument bugs. (2) The sidecar's heal-first policy silently reverted **my own legitimate edits**: an entry inserted into `AGENTS.md` and a test-count sync in three documents were both rolled back by a leaked sidecar, because the mechanism can only see "content differs from the sidecar" and has no way to know *who* made the change. That is worse than the defect it was built to fix — pollution is loud and the audit sees it; **a self-healing mechanism that reverts legitimate work is silent.** Reverted per the cycle rule: `test/_doc-probe-lock.js` back to `HEAD` (sidecar functions remaining: **0**), the new lock deleted, the sidecar directory removed, `AGENTS.md` restored from the commit made earlier this session (82,759 bytes, cycle-19 entry intact) and the cycle-20 entry re-inserted, and the test count synced 1366 → **1359** in README/SKILL/CURRENT_STATE. Gate after revert, measured item by item against this cycle's starting point: verify **15/15**, run-all **1359/0**, audit **84/84**. `src/` unchanged, so MCP was not restarted (401 unauthenticated, still online). **Five lessons, all of them generalisations of ones this file already holds.** (a) *A self-healing mechanism must be able to tell a probe's pollution from a human's edit — otherwise it reverts legitimate work, and that is worse than the pollution it prevents.* The sidecar could only answer "differs", never "who differs". (b) *When you cannot read the holder's identity, treat it as alive: waiting is cheap, two holders are not.* (c) *A scene built outside the lock cannot be asserted to still exist* — my reverse-proof case asserted the pollution was still on disk after `release()`, and a concurrent probe had already healed it, so the proof went red for a reason that was a race rather than a defect. Hold the lock to the last moment. (d) *Raising the lock's wait ceiling exposes a wider race it cannot govern*: 60s → 300s turned the suite 1356/10 because a longer queue means a larger fraction of wall-clock time in which a lock-holding probe is writing `AGENTS.md` while a lock-less plain reader is reading it. That race is real and unfixed; a longer wait only makes it more visible. (e) *A hardcoded anchor goes silently dead once the document is polluted* — `POISON = original.replace('| Engine version | 1.0.0 |', …)` is a no-op when `SKILL.md` already reads `9.9.9`, so the entire lock becomes a check that can never fail, checking for the very pollution it cannot see. The version is now read from `VERSION`. Two defects were located but deliberately left unfixed and are recorded rather than papered over: `audit-doc-coverage.test.js` and `doc-probe-lock-coverage.test.js` do not set `ASPIRA_AUDIT_SKIP_TESTS`, so they spawn the **full suite while holding the doc lock**, and the nested suite's probes then contend for it — one genuine source of the intermittent single-case loss this cycle opened by chasing; and the probe-writes-under-lock / reader-reads-without-lock race above.
- **The angle never asked was the inverse of cycle 10's, and answering it found an instrument that cannot tell "the document is wrong" from "the probe that rewrote the document did not put it back."** Nineteenth cycle, `doc-honest-numbers` again, and the engine chose it decisively this time (0.65, confidence 0.75 — the first discriminated pick since the tie). Cycles 7-12 had asked the audit about its keys, its patterns, its gates, its file set, its measured-vs-patterned keys, and a re-enumeration. The one question never asked is the inverse of cycle 10's *"what files does it scan?"*: **what is in the exemption list?** An exemption is the strongest form of not-measuring — it is a decision to not measure — so the list is where unaudited claims hide by design. Measured first, before touching anything: the audit scans **218** documents and exempts **1,215+**, of which `data/` is 1,120 byte-identical `CORE_VALUES.md` copies (1 distinct content, verified), `docs/` 40, `report/` 8, `plans/` 1, plus 66 name-exempted files (`EXEMPT_NAME_RE`). `skills/` — 246 files, the largest directory after `data/` — turned out to **be** scanned (189 of them), so the angle's first hypothesis was wrong and that was worth establishing rather than assuming. Two real defects came out of it, and the second is much deeper than the first. **The first: an exemption entry that points at a broken symlink.** `HISTORICAL_DOCS` lists `CHAT_LOG_HeartFlow_完整备份_2026-05-16.md`, and it cannot be read. The old code pushed ENOENT and permission errors into the same `exemptUnreadable` bucket, printed one ⚠️ and `continue`d — so the entry still counted in "显式豁免 N 份" while contributing nothing to "不同内容 N 份": **the audit inflated "how much is unaudited" with a document it cannot open.** That is the cycle-10 shape turned inside out — that time 682 copies were counted as *scanned* documents, this time a phantom was counted as an *exempt* one. The truth is better than "the file is missing": it is a **git-tracked symlink** pointing at `/root/mount/CHAT_LOG_HeartFlow_完整备份_2026-05-16_0437a209.md`, a path that exists only in some other environment. `readdirSync` sees it (it does not follow links); `readFileSync` follows it and gets ENOENT. The message now names the cause instead of conflating the two cases. **The second defect is the one worth the cycle, and I caused it while investigating the first.** The audit's main flow is `const m = measure()` (spawns `test/run-all.js` and runs the entire suite) **and only then** `const cl = claims()` (which is where documents are read). The test suite contains probe tests that *rewrite `SKILL.md`, `README.md` and `CURRENT_STATE.md`* — injecting `v9.9.9`, `139 modules` and the like, then restoring. Lines 964-965 of the script already acknowledged this coupling in a comment, but only to explain why `execSync` threw; nobody ever asked **what happens when a probe does not put the document back**. I found out by killing my own 60s-timeout audit runs: SIGTERM propagated audit → `run-all.js` → a probe test, killing it between injection and restore. The pollution stayed on disk, `claims()` read it, and the audit produced **7 mismatches blaming `SKILL.md`/`README.md` for "claiming 9.9.9" and "139 modules"** — none of which the repository claims. **The instrument cannot distinguish "the document is wrong" from "the probe that rewrites the document did not put it back,"** and it blamed the documents. Worse, the thing that runs the polluting tests is the audit itself: **the instrument measures itself through the thing it is measuring.** The fix is to snapshot every document's content *before* spawning, and have `claims()` read only the snapshot; whatever the probes do afterwards is invisible to the audit, which now checks the claims as they were when it started. Verified behaviourally, not just structurally: a `--require` preload hook intercepts the audit's `spawnSync('node', ['test/run-all.js'])`, dirties `SKILL.md` *during* `measure()`, and returns a fake runner summary — with the snapshot in place the audit reports no version mismatch, and the paired negative case (the document genuinely wrong, no hook) proves the check is not vacuous. **A third defect, pre-existing since cycle 10 and invisible until now: the repository's standard comment stripper silently deletes 44% of `audit-doc-numbers.js`.** The stripper (`test/self-diagnostic-paths.test.js:49`) assumes `/*` only appears in block comments. This file has a legal `/**` header on line 1, a `/*` hidden inside a *line comment* on line 57 (`skills/*/SKILL.md`), and a `*/` hidden inside a *string literal* on line 81 (`_tc_*/_cache_probe_*`). The block-comment regex therefore ran from line 1's `/**` all the way to line 81's `*/`: measured **58,528 bytes stripped to 32,679 (55.8% retained)**, and the entire `EXEMPT_DIRS` table — including the `data` entry — was deleted as "comment". **Any source-level lock on this file built on "strip comments first" matches nothing at all** — a check that can never fail, wearing the costume of a working check, which is the shape this file keeps recording. Both literals were rewritten to contain neither `/*` nor `*/`; retention is now a normal 56% and every key symbol survives, with a canary that fails the moment either literal comes back. **And the same cycle paid for a recorded lesson a third time: cleaning the probe pollution with `git checkout -- SKILL.md README.md CURRENT_STATE.md` reverted all three documents to HEAD (`8b2853c`), which predates the cycle 12-18 syncs.** Test counts fell back to 1182, the formula count to 1286, and the `17-layer pipeline*` footnote added two cycles earlier vanished; the only thing that noticed was `layer-count-two-readings.test.js`, a pure reader — exactly as cycle 7 recorded. **`git checkout -- <doc>` is not a safe way to clear probe pollution, because it restores the document to a stale commit rather than to its last-good state, and the pollution and the legitimate content live in the same file.** Suite 1351 → **1359** (`test/audit-self-pollution.test.js`, 8 cases, with live injection in three directions plus two "this lock can fail" proofs); verify 15/15; audit **84/84** (was 85/85 — the count is a function of document content, and no *current* scale claim lost coverage: 110 lines carrying numbers beside scale words were checked and all are historical narrative, code samples, per-file counts or quotations under criticism). `scripts/` and `test/` changed but `src/` did not, so **MCP was not restarted** (still 401 unauthenticated). One process note, same class as everything above: a timed-out command left `scripts/zz-orig.js` behind because its `rm` never ran — **a killed command leaves its debris, and the debris is indistinguishable from source until someone looks.**

- **The largest known gap was 39% vs 100%, the recorded "naive fix" had been measured net-negative, and the reason it failed is that the evasion transform is lossy — so the fix is to match space-tolerantly instead of restoring the text.** Eighteenth cycle, and the first on `adversarial-robustness` since cycle 4; the engine chose it itself (0.61, tie-broken by consequence_value after four options landed on the same score, confidence 0). The gap is the largest single one on record: letter-space evasion recall **39%** (16/41) against plaintext **100%** (41/41). AGENTS.md already recorded that widening `strip_letter_space` to 1–2 letter tokens was tried and **reverted** — 53 benign samples folded to recover 1 malicious one. This cycle re-measured before touching anything, and the control measurement is what reframed the problem: **every one of the 41 malicious samples is caught in plaintext, and zero are missed in both plaintext and evasion**, so all 25 evasion misses are *pure* evasion gaps, not pattern gaps. That matters because it means the fix belongs in the obfuscation layer, not the pattern tables. **Then the first thing I had wrong was the shape of the evasion itself.** I assumed `replace(/([a-z])([a-z])/gi, a+' '+b)` produces a uniform `d i s g u s t i n g n i g g e r` — one long run of single letters. It does not: the pairing is non-overlapping, so the product is **letters grouped in pairs**, `d is gu st in g n ig ge r`, with residual two-letter segments. Two consequences, and the second is the one that decides the architecture: (a) the residual `is`/`gu`/`st` segments break `strip_letter_space`'s "≥3 consecutive single letters" requirement, so the collapse layer never fires at all; (b) more fundamentally, **the transform is lossy** — the space that originally separated `disgusting` from `nigger` is byte-identical to every space the transform inserted, so collapsing yields `disgustingnigger` and the word boundary is unrecoverable, while `/\bnigger\b/` requires exactly that boundary. So the reverted attempt was not a badly-chosen threshold; **it was a dead-end route, and its net-negative measurement was the correct verdict on the wrong approach.** Any "restore then match" design has a ceiling set by the transform itself. The fix is to not restore: compile the term `nigger` to `\bn\s*i\s*g\s*g\s*e\s*r\b` and match it **in place on the original text**, where the surrounding spaces are still spaces and the `\b` anchors still hold. No collapse, no merging, no lost boundary. Measured: letter-space recall **16/41 → 29/41 (39% → 70.7%)**, +13, with plaintext 41/41 and benign FP **0/106** both unchanged, and the other two evasion classes untouched (char-separator 85.4%, HTML entity 95.1%). **Two boundaries were load-bearing and both were learned by getting them wrong.** First, `\s*` matches *zero* spaces, so every space-tolerant pattern is a strict superset of its plaintext form — which means the term list may only contain terms the engine **already blocks in plaintext**, because enabling the tolerance on a benign word converts that word into a false positive. Measured: auto-extracting literals from the pattern tables pulled out `always` and `rather`, and four benign samples were immediately mis-flagged. So `src/shield/letter-space-evasion.js` carries a hand-curated list, restricted to slurs and injection phrases already blocked by `hate_speech`/injection patterns. Second, and the more instructive failure: the signal is *named* "字母间距混淆", so it must not fire on plaintext. My first predicate required whitespace between **every** adjacent letter — which is exactly the uniform-single-letter shape I had wrongly assumed, so the module reported "not detected" for every real evasion. The correct predicate is *at least one* gap in the term's letter sequence. Both boundaries are now locked behaviourally rather than remembered. `test/letter-space-evasion.test.js` (8 cases) locks the pair-grouped shape itself, detection of seven evasion forms, that plaintext `nigger`/`developer mode`/`ignore all previous instructions` are **not** reported by this layer, that benign text including `h a t e speech is unacceptable and wrong` does not fire, that the term list contains no benign words, that the adversarial layer returns `rewrite`, and an eighth case that proves the lock itself can go red by running the *wrong* (uniform-single-letter) predicate and asserting it fails — **a check that can never fail is indistinguishable from no check**. Verified live in three directions: short-circuiting the detector, reverting the predicate to the uniform form, and deleting `nigger` from the term list each turn the lock red *by name*. Live MCP (restarted, 401 unauthenticated, `tools/list` → **181/181**) returns `rewrite` with reason `对抗变体: 词拆分、字母间距混淆(隐藏已知术语)` for `y ou a re a d is gu st in g n ig ge r` and `a ll n ig ge rs m us t d ie`, `block` for the plaintext slur, and `pass` for `hello world` and `今天天气不错`. Suite 1343 → **1351**; verify 15/15; audit **85/85**; AGENTS.md's own 39% evasion reading was corrected to 71% with a pointer to this entry. **The 12 residual misses are disclosed, not counted as solved:** eight are code-semantic samples (`eval(userInput)`, `new Function(userInput)`, `innerHTML`, `document.write`, SQL concatenation, `exec`, an RSA private key, a password assignment) that need code patterns rather than a word list; `women belong in the kitchen 女人就该待在厨房` needs phrase-level tolerance with mixed Chinese/English, which needs its own measured cycle; and `all f_aggot_s must die` belongs to the underscore-separator family, not this one. **One disclosure carried forward from an earlier cycle and confirmed again here:** the adversarial layer is reachable only through `aspira_gate_pipeline` — `aspira_gate_check` on the same evaded text still returns `pass`, because it runs `discriminate()` only. Agents that gate untrusted input with the lightweight tool are still outside this protection.

- **Static tool accounting had been green for three cycles, and calling all 181 handlers once found a gate that could never say "pass" — plus a test that had locked the defect in place by rationalising it.** Seventeenth cycle, and the first after `test-coverage-gap` was marked exhausted; the engine rotated on its own to **`mcp-tool-enhancement`** (0.62/0.65). The measurement was deliberately *behavioural* rather than static: convention #3's three-place rule and tool/handler mutual coverage were already locked by `test/mcp-tool-integrity.test.js` (7 cases, all green), so instead every one of the **181** handlers was actually invoked. First run of that instrument was wrong and had to be corrected before it meant anything: it called the handlers synchronously, so the **async** ones returned a Promise, `JSON.stringify` of a Promise is `{}`, and twelve healthy tools were reported as "returns size 2" while their rejections escaped as unhandled rejections — **an instrument that measures the wrong thing reports healthy code as hollow**. With `await`, the picture is clean: **132 ok / 18 throw "X 是必填参数" (correct validation) / 31 error-obj, of which 29 are also correct missing-parameter messages.** Two genuine defects. **The first was in `src/gate-outbound.js`'s `scanPII`, and it is the recorded shape at its purest:**

```
const findings = [
  { pattern: /邮箱正则/g,   type: 'email',       level: '内部' },
  { pattern: /\b[4-6]\d{15}\b/g, type: 'credit_card', level: '机密' },
  { pattern: /\b[PEG]\d{8}\b/g,  type: 'passport',    level: '机密' },
];
```

Three **pattern descriptors** that nothing ever matched against the text, pushed into the results as if they were findings. Three consequences: (a) each serialises to `{}` — no `id`, `name`, `severity`, `match` or `position`, so the caller receives a batch of **empty objects it cannot act on**; (b) `piiFindings.length` is therefore always ≥ 3, so `checkOutbound`'s `piiFindings.length > 0` branch is always true and **`action` can never be `'pass'`**; (c) real hits are inflated by 3 (measured: 2 real PII reported as `piiCount: 5`). Measured before the fix: `今天天气不错`, `hello world`, `12345` and `'   '` all returned `rewrite` with `reason: "命中 PII 规则 (3 处)"` — **and not one of those 3 existed.** An outbound gate that reports PII for every input is no gate at all, and it looked perfectly healthy because real PII *was* still caught. Fix: `findings` starts empty, and of the three descriptors only **passport** was genuinely uncovered — `email` is equivalent to `PII_RULES.EMAIL` and `\b[4-6]\d{15}\b` is a subset of `PII_RULES.BANK_CARD`, so re-adding either would double-count the same span (the `unsupported_claim`/`pseudo_causal` shape already recorded), while `PASSPORT` is promoted to a real rule so it gets the same `id`/`name`/`severity` as every other rule and enters `maskPII`. **The second defect is the one worth the most, because a test had locked the first one in place:** `test/compliance/compliance.test.js`'s `G3-4` asserted `checkOutbound({ text: '今天天气真好' }).action === 'rewrite'`, with the comment *"实际: 公开内容触发 PII 规则 rewrite（非 bug，是 PII 规则触发）"*. That sentence is **the defect's own product, offered as a justification** — the author mistook the phantom findings for real PII hits, and the assertion then made the broken behaviour load-bearing. It is the same lesson as `G3-3` in the same file, fixed earlier: that time `forcedLevel`'s string/object contract mismatch killed the classification `block` branch, and the test's comment likewise recorded the broken behaviour as "实际". **Twice in one file a comment has argued that a defect is the design.** `G3-4` now asserts the designed behaviour (`pass`, `piiCount: 0`) and keeps its original *intent* — PII present → `rewrite` with sanitisation — using a text that actually contains PII. The second genuine defect was smaller: `aspira_check_outbound` returned a raw `TypeError` message (`Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'toLowerCase')`) where the other 18 missing-parameter tools return a clean `text 是必填参数`, because `checkOutbound`'s parameter was destructured with no default; the parameter is now normalised first (a `= {}` default alone is not enough — it covers `undefined` but not `null`) and validated at entry. `test/outbound-pii-no-phantom.test.js` (5 cases) locks it: `findings` must initialise to `[]`, `pass` must be reachable for PII-free text, `piiCount` must equal the real hit count for four single-PII cases, every finding must carry actionable fields, and a fifth case proves the lock itself can go red by running its own predicate against a known-phantom array — **a check that can never fail is indistinguishable from no check**. Verified live in three directions: re-adding the phantom turns three cases red *by name*, and the live server (restarted, 401 unauthenticated, `tools/list` → **181/181**) now returns `pass`/`piiCount 0` for clean text and `rewrite`/`piiCount 1` for a phone number. Suite 1338 → **1343**; verify 15/15; audit **85/85**; corpus was unchanged (106 benign / FP 0.9%, 41 malicious / recall 100%); `src/gate-outbound.js` changed, so MCP was restarted.

- **Convention #6 had never been enforced by anything, and following "what can the gate not see" found a second instance of the same shape: two self-diagnostic steps that fail 100% of the time, in a diagnostic no test has ever run.** Sixteenth cycle, fourth consecutive `test-coverage-gap`. Convention #6 reads: *"Verify syntax after every edit: `node --check <file>`."* A pure human-discipline rule: measured, **no test in the repository executes it** — `--check` appears exactly once in `test/`, inside a comment in `test/unwired-module-coverage.test.js`. There are **351** JS files under `src/`. The claim was checked rather than assumed: instrumented `Module._load` via `NODE_OPTIONS='--require'`, which the runner's 236 subprocesses inherit, and measured that the whole gate loads **348 of 351** — **29 never loaded, of which 21 are live non-archive modules** (`src/dream/dream-engine.js`, `src/core/sleep-wake.js`, `src/heartflow-api-server.js`, `src/reasoning/verifier.js`, …). A syntax error in any of those 21 is *completely* silent. Proven by injection, and the proof required correcting my own instrument twice: first I measured "uncovered" by grepping test *text* for the module name, and injected into `src/ai-anti-pattern.js` — the suite went **1002/130 red**, because that file *is* loaded by `heartflow.js`; a name not mentioned in tests is not a module not loaded. Second, the decisive run had to **move the new lock out of the tree** to simulate the gate as it was, and with the lock removed, a syntax error in `src/dream/dream-engine.js` left **run-all at 1333/0 and verify at 15/0 — both all-green, with zero mention of the file**. The kicker that makes this the recorded shape rather than a coverage statistic: there *is* a `test/dream-engine.test.js`, and it passes — but it requires `src/dream/engine.js`, **a different file**. So the module looks covered by name. `test/src-syntax-check.test.js` (2 cases) now runs `node --check` over all of `src/`, and its second case is deliberately more important than its first: a deliberately-broken temp file *must* be reported, because **a check that can never fail is indistinguishable from no check** — the deduplicator that printed "811 份全不同、0 重复" while its `crypto.createHash` threw a swallowed `ReferenceError` is the same lesson, and the instrument choice here was corrected for the same reason: `new vm.Script(src)` is 180× faster and reported `src/core/heartflow.js` as *"Illegal return statement"* where `node --check` reports zero errors, because `vm.Script` does not wrap the module and a top-level `return` is legal in CommonJS — **an instrument that reports its own limitation as an engine defect**. Convention #6's own wording is used verbatim, no translation layer. **Then the second gap, found by the same thread and the same shape:** `src/core/self-diagnostic.js`'s 20-step diagnostic has **step 17 and step 18 failing on every single run** — step 17's engine table points **5 of 6 entries at directories that do not exist** (`PsychologyEngine` says `src/core`, real is `src/emotion`; `DreamEngine` says `src/core`, real `src/dream`; `SelfModel` says `src/core/consciousness`, real `src/identity`; `SelfHealingRL` and `LessonBank` say `src/core`, real `src/cortex`), measured `allFound = 1/6` against the step's own pass threshold of `>= 4`; step 18 looked for `dream-engine.js` / `dream.js` / `interactive-dream.js` under `src/core/` when all three live in `src/dream/`, so `found` is always empty. Nothing noticed because **no test anywhere calls `runDiagnostic`** (`grep -rln runDiagnostic test/` → empty): the entire 20-step self-diagnostic has never been executed by the gate. And the most pointed detail is in the old comment above step 17's table — titled *"引擎实际文件位置（已确认）"* and listing *"DreamEngine: dream.js"*, while the `dir` on the very next line still says `src/core`: **the comment knew the correct location and the code used the wrong one.** Both steps were repaired (measured `6/6` and `found: [3 files]`, 20 steps, **0 failing**). The lock is deliberately *source-level* and does not run the diagnostic, because `step19_versionSync` **writes files** — it rewrites `package.json`, `SKILL.md`, `README.md` and `heartflow.js` when versions disagree, so letting a test run `runDiagnostic()` would let the suite rewrite repository source, and under the concurrent runner two processes doing that at once is exactly the cycle-7 probe-pollution defect; the same discipline as *a test that spawns the audit must hold the doc lock* — **anything that can write must not be casually run**. `test/self-diagnostic-paths.test.js` (3 cases) statically核对 the table against disk, guards against vacuous pass by requiring exactly 6 parsed entries, and strips comments first — necessary because the repair wrote the real locations into comments, and an un-stripped lock would read its own documentation as the offence, which is the cycle-14 and cycle-15 trap for the third cycle running. Verified live in three directions: reverting either wrong path is caught *by name*, and a comment-only change still passes. Suite 1335 → **1338**; verify 15/15; audit **85/85**; corpus was unchanged (106 benign / FP 0.9%, 41 malicious / recall 100%); `src/core/self-diagnostic.js` changed, so **MCP was restarted** — 401 unauthenticated, and `tools/list` with the token returns **181/181** `aspira_*` tools. One process failure worth recording because it is the same class: an earlier injection script was killed by its 60s timeout *after* writing a broken module and *before* restoring it, so `src/dream/dream-engine.js` sat broken and the next script silently adopted the broken content as its "backup" — caught only because the restore check compared against `git status` rather than trusting the in-memory copy. **A restore that trusts the same variable that was corrupted is not a restore.**

- **Cycle 14 fixed the mount files that were never counted; following the same thread found 33 test files that were checking nothing at all, and whose failure output was byte-for-byte indistinguishable from success.** Fifteenth cycle, third consecutive `test-coverage-gap`. The thread was: *of all the test files run-all executes, which ones contribute **zero** cases to the total?* Cycle 14's fix made every mount-style file count. Measured next: **32 files still contributed 0 cases**, and none of them was mount-style — all 33 (32 plus one at root) shared one template:

```
const assert = require('assert');          // required, never used
async function run() {
  try {
    const mod = require('../../src/utils/logger.js');
    console.log('PASS logger.test.js (module loads)');
  } catch (e) {
    // Module may have optional deps or initialization requirements
    console.log('SKIP logger.test.js (' + e.code + ': ' + e.message.slice(0, 60) + ')');
  }
}
run().catch(e => console.log('SKIP logger.test.js: ' + e.code));
```

Three consequences stack to make this check **equivalent to not existing**: (1) on load failure it prints `SKIP` and **exits 0** — measured directly by repointing the `require` at a nonexistent module, exit code is 0 in both cases, so *load failure and load success are indistinguishable*; (2) neither the `PASS` nor the `SKIP` line contains 通过/失败, so run-all's `emitResult` regex `/(\d+) 通过, (\d+) 失败/` matches no summary line and the file contributes **0 passes and 0 failures**; (3) it requires `assert` and never calls it — a check that cannot fail by construction. So **if `src/utils/logger.js` were deleted, renamed, or given a syntax error, run-all would still report all-green.** This is not low coverage; it is a thing that claims to be checking while checking nothing, and whose failure mode is *textually identical in shape* to its success mode. It is the recorded shape — **a catch that turns an exception into a plausible answer is more dangerous than no self-check** — except here it is not one catch inside one self-check: it is **one catch replicated across 33 files**, and its failure output (`SKIP ...`) looks exactly like a legitimate test status line. All 33 were converted to mount style (`test('X 模块可加载', () => require(...))`), so a load failure is now 1 counted failure. All 33 loaded successfully before conversion, verified first, so nothing was traded away. `test/no-silent-skip-loadcheck.test.js` (3 cases) mechanically forbids the shape from returning, checks that the converted files really do count ≥1 case with 0 failures, and checks that no "module loads" file requires an unused `assert`. **The lock then failed against its own fix, at 33-file scale**: the conversion script wrote the *old template verbatim* into each repaired file's JSDoc to explain what it replaced, so "this shape must not appear" immediately judged all 33 freshly-repaired files guilty. This is the cycle-14 trap exactly — that time `pickJob`'s JSDoc quoted the removed bypass and an un-stripped source lock read its own documentation as the offence — relearned once, and paid for again one cycle later at 33× the size. Both predicates now strip comments before matching, and the second injection case exists specifically to prove the stripping works. **The lock's scope also had to be corrected:** it initially scanned `archive/` too and went red on 10 dead-test files whose target modules were deliberately deleted. A lock that scans files the runner never executes can be red for reasons that do not affect the gate at all, which trains people to ignore it — so it now scans exactly `collectTestFiles`' live set and **discloses** the 10 archived occurrences in a `[note]` line rather than hiding them or failing on them. Suite 1297 → **1333**; verify 15/15; audit **85/85**; corpus was unchanged (106 benign / FP 0.9%, 41 malicious / recall 100%); no `src/` change, so MCP was not restarted.

- **Convention #4 warned about the wrong thing, and the real gap was one layer earlier: run-all.js bypassed every subdirectory test file before it ever looked at the file's style.** Fourteenth cycle, second consecutive `test-coverage-gap` (the engine chose it again, 0.63/0.7 — the slice is not exhausted). Convention #4 reads: *"Tests must be reachable by `test/run-all.js`. It walks `test/` recursively and executes each file in a subprocess. A test file that requires `../src/...` from a subdirectory must use the correct relative depth, or it will never run."* It warns about **relative depth**. Measured: all **271** non-archive test files *are* collected and *are* executed — depth is not the problem. The problem is **how**: the dynamic loop opened with `if (rel.includes('/')) { jobs.push(subTestJob(...)); continue; }`, so every subdirectory file was run with bare `node <file>` and the style detection below it — mount / arrow-mount / jest, the very detection that had already been repaired once for root-level files at lines 234-238 — **never applied to them at all**. The same rule covered exactly half the files, and that half was every subdirectory. Consequence for a mount-style file: `node <file>` exits 0 with no output, `emitResult` matches no summary line, and the file contributes **0 cases and 0 failures**.
