Measured cost: **39 pre-existing test cases had never entered the total** — `identity/agent-psychology.test.js` (21 `test()` calls; bare → nothing, via `_mount.js` → 21), and two `CORE_TESTS` entries that used `subTestJob` directly and so bypassed detection a second time: `knowledge/classics-value-mapper.test.js` (10) and `agent-boundary-guard.test.js` (8). run-all reported **1253 passing** with 39 of those cases never counted, and 1253 looked entirely normal. **This is the cycle-7 shape at the suite level: a number measured, displayed, and never compared — except here it is starker, because the cases were not merely unmeasured, they were *present, traversed, and silently reduced to zero*.** `classics-value-mapper.test.js` was **doubly** silent: it declared the mount signature `function({ test })`, never called `test()`, built its own `cases` array, and printed `classics-value-mapper: 11 cases passed` — a line containing neither 通过 nor 失败, so even a correct harness would have counted 0. Converting it to real harness usage surfaced a **wrong expectation that had never run**: it asserted `parseHit('no-colon-here').file` is truthy, while the real contract (`src/knowledge/classics-rules.js:1033`) degrades to `{ file: null, line: null, raw }`. **An assertion that has never executed is not an assertion** — it had been green only because it had never run at all. Fixes: one shared `pickJob()` now decides every file's style (CORE_TESTS *and* the dynamic walk), and the file itself now calls `test()` with the expectation corrected to the real degraded shape. `test/subdirectory-test-reachability.test.js` (5 cases) locks it in two layers — a static check that every mount-style file's export body actually calls `test()`, and a behavioural check that each subdirectory mount file yields ≥1 case with 0 failures through `_mount.js`. Both layers were needed: the static check matches the *text* `test(`, so renaming the injected parameter (`function ({ test: _unused })` while still calling `test(...)`) slips past it, and only the behavioural check catches that. The source-level lock that the bypass is gone had to **strip comments first** — `pickJob`'s own JSDoc quotes the removed bypass verbatim to explain what it replaced, and an un-stripped lock treated its own documentation as the offence and would have stayed red until someone deleted the real lock along with it. Suite 1253 → **1297**; verify 15/15; audit **85/85**; corpus was unchanged (106 benign / FP 0.9%, 41 malicious / recall 100%); no `src/` change, so MCP was not restarted.

- **Convention #1 named five places that must agree on the version, and nothing checked any of them.** Thirteenth cycle, and the first after `doc-honest-numbers` was marked exhausted — the engine rotated to **`test-coverage-gap`** on its own, which is where the cross-slice gap recorded in cycles 11 and 12 belonged. Convention #1 reads: *"Never hardcode the version. `VERSION` is the single source of truth. `src/core/version.js` reads it and carries a fallback that must match. `package.json` and `SKILL.md` must match. `hf.version` reads `VERSION` at runtime."* Five named locations. Measured: **`bin/verify.js` checked none of them** (its only version-ish check is the Node major version), **no test called `scripts/sync-version.js`** (it is referenced only in `package.json`), and `scripts/audit-doc-numbers.js` — added in cycle 11 — locks only the *document* claims (`| Engine version | 1.0.0 |` in SKILL.md, `> 版本 | v1.0.0` in CURRENT_STATE.md), which says nothing about `package.json`, the fallback literal, `BUILD_DATE`, or the runtime value. So `sync-version.js` is a **writer with no verifier**: bump `VERSION`, forget to run it, miss any one of the five, and nothing anywhere says a word. All five happened to agree at 1.0.0, which is exactly why this survived — **a consistent system and a locked system look identical until the day they diverge.** `test/version-consistency.test.js` (8 cases) now locks all five, and the same check was added to `bin/verify.js` as a fifteenth item so the convention's own prescribed gate reports it. **The fallback is the case worth the whole cycle, because `src/core/version.js` already documented the hazard in its own comment:** *"兜底值必须与 VERSION 保持同步 —— 否则 VERSION 文件读失败时(打包遗漏/权限问题)引擎会自报一个落后几十个版本的号，**且外部没有任何提示**。"* That is the guard-that-hides-the-failure shape this repository keeps recording: the fallback exists solely for the moment the `VERSION` read fails, and on every normal run the read succeeds, so **the fallback is never evaluated — a wrong fallback produces no output at all.** It only speaks at the worst moment, and at that moment it is silent about being wrong. The test therefore proves the fallback path actually executes rather than merely asserting the literal matches: it copies `version.js` into a temp directory with `safe-fs` stubbed to throw (stubbing rather than copying the real dependency chain, because `safe-fs.js` itself requires `path-guard.js` and the scenario being simulated is "the read fails" — missing file, permission denied, or the guard rejecting the path are one `catch` to `version.js`), and asserts the module falls back to the literal. No repository file is touched, which matters after cycle 11's lesson that a tool reporting success while writing the wrong thing is worse than one that fails. All four injected drifts (`package.json`, `SKILL.md`, the fallback literal, `BUILD_DATE`) turn the suite red by name; verify 14 → **15**; run-all 1245 → **1253**; audit **85/85**; corpus was unchanged (106 benign / FP 0.9%, 41 malicious / recall 100%); no `src/` change, so MCP was not restarted.

- **Re-running the cycle-8 enumeration after the scan set shrank fourfold found the formula library — three documents, three numbers, and zero measurements.** Twelfth consecutive `doc-honest-numbers` cycle. The previous five had asked the audit about its keys (cycle 7), its patterns (cycle 8), its gates (cycle 9), its file set (cycle 10) and its measured-vs-patterned keys (cycle 11), so this cycle re-ran cycle 8's mechanical enumeration rather than inventing a new angle — the earlier conclusion was measured on **798 scanned documents and 2,792 numeric hits**, and both the file set (now **218**) and the key set (six keys added) had since changed, so "we already enumerated this" was itself a stale reading. Of the **623** auditable forms that still produce no claim, the scale-level ones are almost all subdirectory workflow guidance (`500 行`, `164 个 JS/TS`) and historical milestones — **except one**: the formula library. `INSTALL.md` claimed `公式库(2397个)`, `CURRENT_STATE.md` claimed `1286 formulas`, and `formulas/README.md` described `formulas.json` as `未拆分前的完整382条公式文件`. Three documents, three numbers, and **`formulas` appeared zero times in the audit**. Measured: `src/formula/formula-module.js:13`'s `formulasFile` default is `formulas/formulas.json`, which holds **608** entries — so the first claim was inflated ~4×, the second ~2×, and the third was a *pre-split* value that had been overtaken by growth (cognitive_science 119→149, psychology 61→101, philosophy 34→65, physics 12→95, mathematics 84→126). **A stale reading is harder to catch than a wrong one, because it was once correct.** The patterns deliberately lock only the *total*: `formulas-core.json` (284) and `formulas-archive.json` (98) are per-file counts, both measured true, and matching them against 608 would manufacture false alarms — the same confusion between two different quantities in one family that has produced instrument failures before. **Then the new keys broke two existing regression locks in a way worth recording, because it is the misleading-failure shape this file keeps documenting.** `test/audit-gate-scope.test.js`'s `作用域计数不得被当成引擎总量` and `行内代码跨度内的数字…` both asserted `assertEqual(mismatchCount(out), 0)`, and their own comment explained why: *"「当前文档 0 不符」就是 notScoped 的回归锁"*. The moment three documents were found to have genuinely wrong formula numbers, both turned red — with failure messages pointing at `notScoped` and `inCodeSpan`, while the real cause sat in `INSTALL.md`. **A regression lock that uses "the whole audit is green" as a proxy for "my gate works" is coupled to every future new key**, and it fails by naming the wrong suspect. Both are now targeted: they assert only that the specific phrases their gate is responsible for (`Tier-2 module`, `tier 1 modules`, `across 8 modules`, the quoted `1216`) never enter the mismatch list — so removing `notScoped` still fails them, but an unrelated wrong document no longer does. Suite 1239 → **1245** (`test/formula-count-measured.test.js`, 6 cases, with live injection in all three documents plus a regression lock that the per-file counts are never matched against the total); verify 14/14; audit **85/85**; scanned documents 218 (217 distinct, 1 duplicate); corpus was unchanged (106 benign / FP 0.9%, 41 malicious / recall 100%); no `src/` change, so MCP was not restarted.

- **The audit had never been asked which *files* it scans, and the answer inflated its own
  coverage number fourfold.** Tenth consecutive `doc-honest-numbers` cycle. The previous
  three cycles asked the audit about its keys (cycle 7: "what was never measured?"), its
  patterns (cycle 8: "what numeric forms exist?"), and its gates (cycle 9: "which of my own
  rules can never let a claim through?"). This cycle asked the one question never asked:
  **what is in the file set?** Measured answer: the audit self-reported *"已扫文档 904 份"* —
  but `data/` held **682 byte-identical copies of `CORE_VALUES.md`**, one per
  `_tc_<ts>_<rand>/` and `_cache_probe_<ts>/` directory, all leaked test cache/probe
  artifacts. Real distinct documents: **222**. The self-reported number was **4× inflated**,
  and **6,192 of the 28,592 raw numeric forms (21.7%)** came from those copies, so the
  coverage percentage's numerator and denominator were both polluted by one 332-byte
  constitution containing nine list ordinals and **zero** engine-scale claims. Same family as
  the recorded "22.3% only counted the root directory, so the self-report was itself
  optimistic" — opposite direction: that time the denominator was too small, this time the
  denominator held things that were not the denominator. **A count that reports copies as
  documents is worse than no count, because it makes coverage look broader than it is.**
  `data/` is now exempt with a written reason (runtime data, not documentation), and the
  self-report additionally prints **distinct-content** and **byte-duplicate** counts, because
  a number that can silently re-inflate is a number that will. **A second artifact surfaced
  in the same probe**: a directory in the repo root literally named `"`, containing an entire
  quoted absolute path tree (`"/var/folders/…/T/aspira-mx-D4BRqZ"/` with its own
  `CORE_VALUES.md`, `.opencode/memory/heartflow_state.json`, `data/agent-card.json`). Its
  entry point was already fixed in an earlier cycle (`resolveHFDir()` returned
  `process.env.HEARTFLOW_DIR` unvalidated, so a quoted value silently built a quoted tree;
  both copies now run `sanitizeHFDir()`), `.gitignore` carries `*aspira-mx-*`, and the
  leaked tree was **never cleaned up** — the audit kept scanning it. `walkMd` now skips
  directories whose names contain quotes, backticks, or control characters: a legitimate
  documentation directory never has a quote in its name. **And the deduplication I added to
  make the self-report honest was itself silently broken.** It read
  `catch (_) { nDistinct++; continue; }` while `crypto` was never imported, so
  `crypto.createHash` threw a `ReferenceError` that the catch swallowed, and every file fell
  into the "count as distinct" branch — the output read *"811 份全不同、0 重复"*, which looks
  exactly like a working deduplicator and is a deduplicator that never ran once. A catch that
  turns an exception into a plausible answer is more dangerous than no deduplication at all:
  it makes a broken self-check display as passing. Both the import and the loud failure are
  now locked. Suite 1227 → **1233** (`test/audit-scan-set-hygiene.test.js`, 6 cases, with
  live injection: a directory named with a quote must not increase the scanned-document
  count, and must be reported as a leaked artifact); verify 14/14; audit **78/78**; scanned
  documents **904 → 218 (217 distinct, 1 duplicate)**; raw numeric forms **28,592 → 1,242**;
  corpus was unchanged (106 benign / FP 0.9%, 41 malicious / recall 100%); no `src/` change, so
  MCP was not restarted.

- **The audit's third angle was to ask which of its own rules can never let a claim
  through — and the answer was that the README front page, the repo's most prominent
  claims line, was unaudited in its entirety.** Ninth consecutive `doc-honest-numbers`
  cycle. The previous two cycles asked the *documents* ("what was never measured?" →
  `domains`; "what numeric forms exist?" → `benign`/`malicious`). This cycle asked the
  *audit*: **which of my own gates can never pass?** Probing README's front page —
  `54 discrimination dimensions × 17-layer pipeline* × 132 modules × 181 MCP tools ×
  1,023 dispatch routes × 1216 passing tests × 0 runtime dependencies` — number by
  number gave: `0 runtime dependencies` → **swallowed by `selfRef`**; `1216 passing
  tests` → **swallowed by `selfRef`**. Meanwhile the *same* number in the table below
  (`| Test suite | 1216 passing`) was audited. The cause: `selfRef` looks for
  `aspira|新愿|heartflow|引擎|本仓库|判别|discriminator` only in the text *before* the
  number, and that line's context contains none of them — **of course it doesn't: the
  README is the engine describing itself, and it need not shout its own name inside its
  own house.** So the most visible line in the repository, including the headline design
  principle "zero runtime dependencies", was outside the audit altogether. Same family as
  the 29th failure, but worse: that time one pattern was eaten; this time **a whole class
  of document (the engine's self-description) was systematically excluded**, and 53/53
  green only meant "the 53 it knows about are right". The fix is **not** to delete
  `selfRef` (that reintroduces the subdirectory false positives it was built for) but to
  narrow its scope to the problem it was built to solve: it guards *subdirectory* docs —
  generic specs, case descriptions, upgrade examples whose numbers are not engine scale.
  Top-level self-description documents exist to describe this engine, so a scale claim in
  them is a claim about this engine *by construction*. **Then a second gate was needed,
  because widening the first immediately flooded in local counts**: `Never require a
  Tier-2 module at the top of heartflow.js`, `At low effort only tier 1 modules run`,
  `11 refs across 8 modules`. All three are about aspira but none is about aspira's
  *total*. `selfRef` answers "is this claim about aspira?"; `notScoped` answers "is it
  about aspira's **total**?" — a number preceded by a scoping word (`tier`, `across`,
  `only`, `each`…) is a local quantity, not an inventory. **And the same probing exposed a
  third, subtler shape**: the key existed, the pattern existed, and the claim was still
  invisible because the word order was reversed — the docs write `1216 passing tests`
  while every pattern expected `tests passing`. Rounds 7 and 8 fixed "no such key"; this
  round found **"the key and the pattern both exist and that one phrasing still never
  matches"**, which looks like coverage and is not. The new pattern immediately surfaced
  a stale `165 passing tests` in SKILL.md — a *historical* reading ("were silently never
  counted", describing the old runner's blind spot), which `narrativeQuote` missed because
  it only inspected the text *before* the number and the past-tense verb sat *after* it.
  Suite 1216 → **1227** (`test/audit-gate-scope.test.js`, 9 cases, plus
  `test/doc-probe-lock-coverage.test.js`, 2 cases); verify 14/14;
  audit **76/76**; corpus was unchanged (106 benign / FP 0.9%, 41 malicious / recall 100%);
  no `src/` change, so MCP was not restarted. **And I re-committed the cycle-56
  concurrency defect in my own new test**: `作用域计数不得被当成引擎总量` spawned the
  audit *without* holding `_doc-probe-lock`, so under the concurrent runner it read another
  test's probe injection and reported a mismatch that did not exist. Direct run: 8/8 green.
  Under `run-all`: 1 failure, pointing at an assertion unrelated to the real cause. A test
  that spawns the audit must hold the lock — the same rule, relearned. **I violated it a
  third time in the very next test I wrote** (`行内代码跨度内的数字是被引用的字面量`), so the
  rule is now locked *mechanically*: `test/doc-probe-lock-coverage.test.js` scans every test
  file, finds the helper that spawns `audit-doc-numbers.js`, and requires every one of its
  call sites to sit inside a `withDocLock` callback. That guard was itself wrong twice
  before it worked — it matched a generic `execFileSync('node', [SCRIPT]` and pulled in two
  tests that run *other* audit scripts (`mcp-contract-audit.js`, `mcp-echo-audit.js`), and it
  inspected the spawn *statement* instead of the helper's *call sites*, so every compliant
  file was reported as an offender. Verified live: removing one lock makes it name the exact
  file and line. A rule I keep relearning is a rule the suite should enforce, not one I
  should remember.
- **The audit's own blind spot was found by enumerating the key set against every numeric
  form the documents actually contain — and the new instrument then failed in the opposite
  direction, swallowing a wrong claim instead of reporting it.** Eighth consecutive
  `doc-honest-numbers` cycle. The previous cycle ended by recommending rotation and leaving
  one concrete direction: *mechanically enumerate* the audit's key set against every numeric
  form the docs contain, instead of asking a human. Done. Of 2,792 numeric hits across 798
  scanned docs, the patterns covered **121 (4.3%)**; almost all of the rest is noise
  (`ms`, `KB`, `Phase`, `Astra`), but filtering to engine-scale forms surfaced
  `benign` / `malicious` — and **the corpus size is the denominator of both the 0.9% FP and
  the 100% recall**, so a wrong denominator silently distorts two percentages at once while
  nothing reports it. A wrong denominator is worse than a wrong percentage: the percentage at
  least gets doubted, the denominator is treated as known. Measured by parsing
  `calibrate-fp-recall.js`'s array literals — **not** by requiring it, because that script's
  top level runs the whole calibration and writes to `data/feedback/`; measuring something
  means first checking what it does as a side effect. **Then the new instrument failed the
  way this slice had never failed before.** Injecting a wrong value (against a measured 106) produced
  **no report at all**. Traced layer by layer: `selfRef` looks for
  `aspira|新愿|heartflow|引擎|本仓库|判别|discriminator` only in the text *before* the
  number, and the sentence is *"measures it on a hand-written labelled corpus of a wrong count"* — the subject is `it`, so not one self-reference word appears literally, so
  `reject` passed the claim through and the wrong number was **silently skipped**. That is
  the mirror image of a false positive: a false positive reports a true thing wrongly —
  loud but visible; **a miss swallows a false thing quietly — and the whole value of an
  audit is that it misses nothing.** A gate that eats wrong claims is worse than no gate.
  `ENGINE_SELF` now also recognises the engine's self-assessment vocabulary
  (`false-positive` / `recall` / `corpus` / `calibrat` / `labelled`), and `NARRATIVE` gained
  the growth/increment forms (`grew by`, `additional`, `to recover`, `from N to N`) that the
  widening immediately exposed — *"The corpus grew by 16 benign … and 7 malicious"* and
  *"folded 53 additional benign samples to recover 1 malicious one"* describe how the corpus
  once grew, not what it is now. **The second fix is an enumeration, and an enumerating
  filter leaks once per new way of narrating.** Declared as residual risk in the source
  comments and here, not papered over. Suite 1210 → **1216** (`test/corpus-size-measured.test.js`,
  6 cases, with live injection: a wrong corpus number must turn the audit red); verify 14/14;
  audit **51/51**; corpus was unchanged (106 benign / FP 0.9%, 41 malicious / recall 100%);
  no `src/` change, so MCP was not restarted.
- **`self-view.json` 的计数曾经一次 `think()` 被计两次，并发下还会丢——前者已修，后者只修了一半。**
  `src/core/think-pipeline.js:190` 与 `src/core/heartflow.js` 里各有一处自增
  `thinkCount`/`lowConfCount`/`blockedCount`，于是串行 think 5 次得 10、think 10 次得 20，
  正好翻倍；6 个进程各 think 一次得 12（期望 6）。计数现已只保留 pipeline 一处，
  heartflow 那处退化为只落盘 + 喂 Reflector，并顺带统一了 `lowConfCount` 的阈值
  （原先 pipeline 用 `conf<0.3`、heartflow 用 `conf<0.4`，同一次 think 会因阈值不同被分别计一次）。
  并发丢失那一半**没有**修好：原实现是「内存态整份覆写」，启动时读一次旧文件、
  之后每次 think 把自己累加后的整份 `_selfView` 覆写回去，多进程并发时后写覆盖先写。
  实测 6 进程同时各 think 10 次（每次间 120ms 拉宽窗口）丢失 67%–83%。
  现已改为「写前重读磁盘 + 合并 + 临时文件 rename」，修掉了「读者读到写了一半的文件」
  这一类，但取计数器 max 只能取到「走得最远的那份」，无法还原总量——**残余丢失仍在**。
  彻底解法是 append-only 增量日志或文件锁；增量日志试过，因与既有的
  「启动载入视图」语义双算（`thinkCount` 飙到 1700 万）而回退。
  `test/self-view-counting.test.js` 锁住已修的部分，并把残余风险写在测试注释里而不是假装修好。


## GitHub

https://github.com/mark-cell-520/aspira

- **The "these 8 need code-semantic patterns, not a word list" verdict was itself wrong, and proving the lock could go red took a second attempt because my first mutation never mutated.** Thirty-second cycle, and the engine chose fp-recall-calibration (composite 0.72, confidence 0.55) — a fourth slice in as many cycles, which is what the rotation is supposed to look like. The slice's largest disclosed gap was letter-space evasion recall at **29/41 (71%)**, the worst of the three measured classes, and cycle 18 had already recorded why the remaining 12 could not be recovered: folding was net-negative (53 benign samples folded to recover 1 malicious one), the transform is **lossy** (the original inter-word space and every inserted space are the same character, so "disgusting nigger" collapses to "disgustingnigger" and the word boundary that the slur pattern needs is unrecoverable), and therefore *any restore-then-match scheme has a ceiling fixed by the transform itself*. Cycle 18's answer was to match known terms **space-tolerantly on the original text** instead, which is `src/shield/letter-space-evasion.js`. That module's own comment then said the 8 code/secret cases left over — `eval`, `new Function`, `innerHTML`, `document.write`, SQL concatenation, `exec`, the RSA private key, `password` — "need code-semantic patterns, not a word list". **That verdict is wrong, and it held the gap open for fourteen cycles.** The module already has the context anchor a bare word list lacks: its two-stage test requires the term to appear in order **and** at least one word inside the matched span to genuinely contain whitespace. Plaintext `exec` / `password` / `select` / `eval` therefore cannot be reported (the whitespace test on "exec" is false), the word-boundary anchors on both ends keep `exec` off `execute`/`executive` and `select` off `selection`, and a space-split word is close to inexpressible in natural prose — so stage 2 *is* the anchor, and it is a stronger one than a syntax anchor like an opening parenthesis would have been. Adding the nine terms takes letter-space evasion **29/41 (71%) → 39/41 (95%)**, recovering ten samples; the corpus reads **identical item for item** (41 malicious / 41 intercepted / 100.0%; 106 benign / 1 escalated / **FP 0.9%, unchanged** — still the same single calibrated-uncertainty sample, with **zero** new false positives), and the other two evasion classes are untouched (separator 35/41 / 85%, HTML 39/41 / 95%). **One concrete trap worth recording:** SQL had to be the single word `SELECT`, not the phrase `SELECT FROM`, because after the transform the original "SELECT * FROM" becomes letters split by spaces with the asterisk still sitting between the two words, while the compiler demands one-or-more whitespace there, so the phrase can never match (measured). That is the enumerated-phrase shape this file has recorded several times: **a phrase built from the un-transformed text is a phrase the transform silently invalidates.** The two remaining letter-space bypasses are disclosed, not hidden: a multi-word mixed Chinese/English phrase ("women belong in the kitchen" plus its Chinese gloss), which needs phrase-level tolerance and its own mixed-script measurement, and an underscore-separated slur, which belongs to the separator family and not to this module's. **The process note is the one this file should have learned already.** My mutation proof — remove the password term, assert the lock goes red — did not mutate anything: that term shares a line with the RSA key term, so the replacement string matched nothing, the file was byte-identical, and the suite printed 10/10 exactly as it does when the lock is healthy. **A mutation proof that does not mutate is indistinguishable from a lock that cannot fail**, and I nearly filed the second as the first. Redone with an assertion on the anchor (fail loudly if the term string is not found), the mutation removes the term for real, the new case fails **by name** at 9/10, and restoring returns 10/10. The rule now written into the proof itself: the mutation must assert that it took effect before its result is believed. Gate measured against this cycle's starting point: verify **15/15**, run-all **1399 → 1401** with 0 failures, audit **86/86 with exit code 0** after syncing the test count across four sites, corpus identical to baseline; `src/` changed, but only inside the space-tolerant term table — no MCP tool definition, handler mapping or permission contract was added or modified, so the tool inventory and permission contract are unchanged and MCP was **not** restarted (recorded as not-performed, not as performed).
- **A rule written for build output ate the source code, and the thing it ate had never been in git — so no git signal could ever have reported it missing.** `mcp-tool-enhancement`, 193rd cycle, and the engine chose the same slice as cycle 192 (0.74/0.65). The cycle opened by re-running the 181-handler census rather than trusting the last one, and the first probe was **wrong in the instrument's usual direction**: passing placeholder args (`{action:'probe', mode:'probe', text:'probe'}`) reported `ok 146 / errorObj 24 / threw 11`, and 21 of those 24 error-objs were the *correct* missing-parameter validation. A probe that does not fill each tool's `inputSchema` required fields measures the validator, not the tool. Filtering to the one that survived a real argument left `aspira_benchmark_run → Cannot find module '.../src/benchmark/benchmark-runner.js'`. **The instinct that paid off was cycle 192's own question asked in the opposite direction: not "is the implementation somewhere else?" but "was it ever here?"** It was. An earlier journal records these two modules *implemented, tested, and measured live* — `aspira_benchmark_run` returning `accuracy=1 total=2`, the suite at 954/0 with 8 new cases. So this is not "never implemented" (cycle 192's shape, where a diagnosis claimed absence over a thing sitting one directory away) but its mirror: **implemented, working, then silently evaporated**. The root cause is one line. `.gitignore:39` held an unanchored `benchmark/` rule. Git's directory patterns match at **any** depth, so `src/benchmark/` — a source directory — was ignored wholesale. The three modules inside it therefore never entered git, which means `git status` could not show them, `git diff` could not show their disappearance, and `git checkout` could never bring them back. **The version-control system was structurally blind to the loss, and that is why it survived: a file that was never tracked cannot produce a "deleted" signal.** Downstream the symptoms were three red test files (`benchmark-modules`, `failure-importer-jsonl-contract`, `recovered-modules`), two advertised MCP tools that could never work, and a dead CLI command (`bin/cli.js:511` requires the same missing file) — and every one of those was recorded in journal after journal as "既有" (pre-existing), i.e. **the symptom was logged as static background for a dozen cycles while nobody asked why the files were gone**. The fix has three parts. (a) The three modules were rewritten against the contract their own tests already lock word for word — `BenchmarkRunner` (`packs` / `loadDirectory` / `runAll` / `flushFailuresToRL`, accuracy genuinely computed so an all-wrong pack scores 0), `FailureCaseImporter` (`parseCases` JSONL and single-line branches, `importFromFile` never throws), `LatencyBenchmark` (CLI's `benchmark` command). (b) `.gitignore:39` became `/benchmark/`, anchored to the repo root, where the rule's legitimate intent (ignoring root-level benchmark output) still holds. Measured both directions: root `benchmark/probe.txt` is still ignored, all three `src/benchmark/*.js` files are not. (c) `test/gitignore-src-anchor.test.js` (4 cases) locks the general form — every unanchored directory rule must not match any existing `src/` subdirectory, every `src/` subdirectory must really be in git, the three recovered files must exist and be un-ignored, plus a reverse proof that injecting `shield/` is caught (a lock that cannot go red is not a lock). **Two errors of my own, both caught by measurement and both worth the generalisation.** (i) My first `.gitignore` fix wrote the explanation as an *inline* `#` comment after the rule — and in gitignore syntax `#` only starts a comment at the **beginning of a line**; inline it becomes part of the pattern. Measured: root `benchmark/` stopped being ignored at all. **A comment syntax is not portable between the formats you work in**, and the probe caught it in one line because the check was bidirectional. (ii) The existing lock `模块不得有顶层 require` had declared `let depth = 0` and never used it — it matched `^(const|let|var)` line by line without regard to nesting, so my function-local `require('./failure-importer.js')` was reported as a top-level require. That is a lock reporting its own legal code as an offence, which trains people to ignore locks. It is now a real `findTopLevelRequires()` that tracks brace depth on a comment-and-string-stripped copy, and it carries its own falsifiability case — and building that case exposed a third, subtler error in *my* detector: the first version stripped strings before matching `require('...')`, so it could no longer see what was being required and the reverse proof went red immediately. The two derivations now do one job each (`noComments` matches requires, `noStrings` counts braces), and the one boundary the detector still gets wrong — a `{` inside a regex literal polluting depth — is **pinned as a disclosed limitation rather than asserted as correct**. **Then the audit exposed a loop this slice had never met, and it is worth naming because it looks like a flake.** Syncing the test count to the run's reading produced **18 failures**, all in the audit family; the audit reported `README.md claims failing tests = 12, measured 18`. The mechanism: the documented failure count is *read out of* `run-all`, and part of that failure count *is* the audit-family tests failing because the documented count is wrong. The loop has exactly one fixed point — **zero failures** — because only then does "what the document says" and "what the suite measures" agree without either depending on the other. Sync to 1890/0 and the loop closed: `run-all` **1890 passed / 0 failed / 1890 total**, verify 15/15, audit re-measured clean. The B-class anchor cascade fired for the **tenth** time (148/153/156/159/167/170/171/179/191/193): three new `src/` modules moved the sweep **380 → 383** and C **269 → 272** while B held at 99 (all three are referenced by the tests that already locked them); SKILL.md's two coverage readings were synced and the audit family returned to baseline. Suite **1883 → 1890**, of which +9 are the repaired `benchmark-modules` cases and +4 the new anchor lock. `src/` changed, but only three new files under `src/benchmark/` — no MCP tool definition, handler mapping or permission contract was added or modified (the two handlers already pointed at these paths; they were simply dead), so MCP was **not** restarted (recorded as not-performed). **One correction to an older record, because it is the same lesson:** a journal several cycles back attributed the `fs.existsSync` invalid-argument `DeprecationWarning` to `benchmark-runner`. Re-measured with `--trace-deprecation`, it originates at **`src/knowledge/classics-rules.js:982`** via `pipeline.js:113`. The earlier attribution named a file that did not exist at the time it was written — the same "diagnosis points at the wrong neighbour" shape, recorded here so the wrong attribution does not outlive the thing it was wrong about. **And the loop above had a second, independent cause that only surfaced after the first was closed: a probe left "The engine ships only 131 modules in total." sitting in `README.md`.** Closing the zero-failure fixed point was not enough, because the audit's next run reported a *new* mismatch — `README.md claims modules = 131, measured 132` — and that line is not a claim this repository has ever made; it is `domains-title-vs-table`'s live-injection probe, unrestored. So the loop has **two entrances, and they are different in kind**: one is arithmetic (the documented count depends on the measured count, which depends on the documented count), the other is *a writer that did not put the document back*, which the arithmetic can never fix because the pollution is not a wrong number — it is a number nobody ever claimed. This is cycle 20/22's unrestored-probe shape arriving through a door those cycles did not have: drift detection was built precisely so pollution would have to speak, and here it spoke, and the report still named `README.md` rather than the probe. The rule that falls out of it: **when a documented count enters a feedback loop, audit for unrestored probes before touching the count**, because a polluted document generates fresh mismatches forever no matter how many times the number is synced. The line was removed by anchor (not by `git checkout`, which cycle 19 measured as reverting to a stale commit), the audit re-measured **93/93 with 0 mismatched**, and the suite returned to **1890/0**. A third reading of the same instability fell out in between and is recorded rather than smoothed over: one run gave 1887/1/1888 with `mcp-check-single-dimension-names` red, and that file is 5/5 green alone — the known concurrency artefact, whose root cause AGENTS.md has located but not closed.
- **The severity table declared `autoRetry` five times and nothing ever read it — and the second criterion it should have replaced was quietly wrong about the most common timeout phrasing.** `test-coverage-gap`, 194th cycle, and the engine chose it on its own (0.77/0.70), which is the rotation working. The cycle opened on cycle 191's standard for picking a module inside class B — *requiring a module one by one is coverage theatre; look for the one whose contract is crisp, round-trippable, and **which someone has already recorded as broken*** — and asked a question that standard had not: **which B-class module does a document already promise by full path?** `coverage-sweep.js` measured 383 modules / 12 suspected dead / 98 alive-but-untested / 273 referenced, identical to SKILL.md, so the gap was inside class B. Cross-referencing the B list against AGENTS.md/README/SKILL found **11** modules named in the docs, and exactly one named by **complete path**: `src/cortex/self-healing.js`, in the "Decision routing" section, as *"Retry or give up? `src/cortex/self-healing.js` — severity-based: low retries, high escalates."* Probing it by **calling** it (not by requiring it) found the defect in one line. `shouldRetry()` read `normalizeError().transient`, a **second, independent criterion** (`/timeout|econnreset|eagain|temporar|busy|rate limit|429|throttle/i`) that ran parallel to the `SEVERITY_MAP` table `classifyFailure()` used. Measured divergence on ten real shapes: `request timed out` and `operation timed out after 30s` — **the two most common ways a timeout is ever written, because they are the spaced forms** — classified `transient` while `shouldRetry` said `false`; `econnreset` classified `medium` while `shouldRetry` said `true`. So the half of AGENTS.md's own promise that says "low retries" was dead for the most frequent input, and `recover()` handed back a structurally valid, self-contradicting answer: `canRetry:false` beside `severityAction:"retry"`, beside a hint reading *reduce the failure surface and retry once*, beside `next_step:"repair"`. **And the field that was supposed to decide this was sitting right there, fully written and fully maintained:** `FailureSeverity` declares `autoRetry` on all five levels, with Chinese descriptions — and `grep -rn autoRetry src/ test/` matched **only those five definition lines**. Never read. Not once. That is the cycle-191 `getFormulaBridge` shape exactly (*a call that is never made*), cycle 179's `_makeScoreResult` (*a guard that is itself the broken thing*), and cycle 171's twin, and it is the most expensive shape this file records because **a written-but-unread field produces no error, no warning, and no test failure — it just quietly means the logic beside it is the real logic.** The fix is one function body: `shouldRetry` now returns `severity.autoRetry === true` after the attempt-ceiling check, so the criterion exists in exactly one place. Measured: all ten shapes now agree (`severity` / `autoRetry` / `shouldRetry`), the attempt ceiling still binds (2 of 2 → true, 3rd → false), and `recover()` returns coherent answers for all four classes. `test/self-healing-severity-contract.test.js` (7 cases) locks it: source-level that `autoRetry` is read inside `shouldRetry` and `.transient` is not; the ten-shape table pinned against expected levels with a note that a deliberate `SEVERITY_MAP` change must update it; the three measured divergences as direct locks; `recover()`'s self-consistency including a hint filter that had to distinguish *"record full error context before any retry"* (a precondition — legal) from *"pause and retry with exponential backoff"* (an incitement — illegal when `canRetry` is false); the attempt ceiling; a reverse proof that inlines the old criterion and requires it to diverge on at least one shape (so the source-level requirement is not decoration); and a final case that **pins the known false positives as disclosed limitations rather than fixing them** — `/oom/i` has no word boundary, so `Conference room is booked` is classified `critical` and `author of the paper` is `high`. That is a pattern-layer limitation of the same family as the obfuscation caveat already recorded, repairing it means touching all nine `SEVERITY_MAP` patterns and re-running the corpus, and it is explicitly **not** this cycle's one minimal change. **Three errors in my own lock, all caught by measurement.** (i) The hint filter first matched `/retry/i` alone and immediately flagged `record full error context before any retry` — a *precondition*, not an incitement — as an illegal hint on a `fatal crash`. (ii) An Edit that rewrote the filter block silently dropped the `if (!c.canRetry)` guard that scoped it, so the assertion then ran against every case including the retryable ones and failed on `reduce the failure surface and retry once` — **a correct hint reported as a defect**. (iii) The reverse proof first tried to load the broken implementation from a temp directory, and failed on `Cannot find module './self-healing-rl.js'`: that module requires `safe-fs`, `atomic-write`, `formula-registry` and `cognitive-bridge`, so reproducing it in `/tmp` means copying half of `src/`. Rewritten as a logically equivalent proof — inline the old criterion and require it to diverge — with the reason written into the comment so nobody re-attempts the file-copy route. **Then the gate produced a reading this slice had never seen, and it took most of the cycle to establish it was not mine.** Syncing the test count to the run's reading produced 18, then 21, then 6 failures — every one of them an audit-family file dying with `Command failed` before printing a summary, so contributing **zero** cases and shrinking the reported total by exactly their case count. Each failing file was 4/4–9/9 green in isolation. The cause was located in the process tree, not the code: `ps -eo pid,ppid` showed the run-all's parent was **another session** — the daily GitHub-sync task — running `node test/run-all.js` at the same moment. Two concurrent suites contend for `test/_doc-probe-lock.js`, the audit tests time out acquiring it, and the audit they spawn then reports a test-count mismatch against the documents, which reddens the audit tests again. The loop has the shape cycle 193 recorded (documented count ← measured count ← audit-family pass/fail ← documented count) but a **different entrance**: cycle 193's was arithmetic plus an unrestored probe; this one is *a second writer*. Waiting for the other session's run to exit and then running once gave **1897 / 0 / 1897 with exit 0**, verify 15/15, audit **93/93 with 0 mismatched**, and the B-class anchor cascade fired for the **eleventh** time (148/153/156/159/167/170/171/179/191/193/194): moving `self-healing.js` from B to C took B **99 → 98** and C **272 → 273**, and SKILL.md's two coverage readings were synced. Suite **1890 → 1897**, all seven new cases from the new lock. **One process note that cost the most and is recorded because it is this file's most-repeated lesson in a new costume:** my own `cp src/cortex/self-healing.js /tmp/sh194-fixed.js && git checkout -- src/cortex/self-healing.js && mv <new test> /tmp/hold194/` chain broke at the third command (`/tmp/hold194` did not exist), which meant the first two had already run — and when I later trusted `/tmp/sh194-fixed.js` as "the fixed version" it turned out to hold the **mutation-applied** copy, i.e. the defect. The file was restored by re-applying the fix from the edit record rather than from the backup, and the new test file — which `find` could not locate and which I briefly recorded as lost — was in fact still in `test/` the whole time; the `ls` that "confirmed" its absence was silenced by `2>/dev/null` inside an `&&` chain that then short-circuited the `mv`. **A backup is not a backup until you have read it back, and a silenced `ls` is not evidence of absence.** Gate: verify **15/15**; run-all **1890 → 1897 / 0 / 1897**, both channels empty; audit **exit 0, 93 claims / 93 consistent / 0 mismatched**; corpus untouched (137 benign / 62 malicious — this cycle changed no pattern, only which criterion decides retry). `src/` changed in one function body plus one new test file — no MCP tool definition, handler mapping or permission contract was added or modified, so MCP was **not** restarted (recorded as not-performed).
- **Three names that never existed, chained across two modules without a single throw — and the one that was supposed to be the six-level consciousness table was `undefined` in a module whose header advertises six-level scoring.** `test-coverage-gap`, 195th cycle, and the engine chose the same slice as cycle 194 (0.77/0.70). Cycle 194 had ended by handing off the audit-family doc-lock contention as the gate's largest instability source; this cycle ignored it deliberately, because that is infrastructure and the slice's own claim had not yet been re-measured. It was: `coverage-sweep.js` read 383 modules / 12 suspected dead / **97 alive-but-untested** / 274 referenced. The B-class-picking standard from cycles 191 and 194 — *find the one whose contract is crisp, round-trippable, and which someone has recorded as broken* — had one candidate left by full path and none by recorded defect, so this cycle used a third question: **which B-class module exports pure functions and constant tables, so its contract can be verified by calling it rather than by requiring it?** `src/dream/dream-loop.js` (745 lines) exports exactly that — `tokenize`, `scoreFragment`, `buildDreamFragments`, `generateDream`, `getDreamEngine`, plus seven constant tables. Probing it by **calling** it found the defect in the export list, not the logic: **`LEVELS` was exported and its value was `undefined`.** Tracing one line up: `dream-loop.js:25` read `const { DreamV11: DreamEngine, LEVELS, DEFAULT_SCORING } = require('./dream.js')`, and `dream.js` exports twelve names — `DreamV5`..`DreamV11`, `DREAM_FUNCTIONS`, `createDreamV5`..`createDreamV11` — **with neither `LEVELS` nor `DEFAULT_SCORING` among them, and no definition of either anywhere in the file.** A destructure that names something the module does not export does not throw; it yields `undefined`. That is cycle 25's exact shape (`PROBE_AUTH_VERSION` vs the exported `PROBE_VERSION`, which wrote `| Engine version | undefined` into a document), and here it chained through **three** points: ① the destructure produced `undefined`; ② `module.exports` listed `LEVELS`, so every caller received a **constant table that looked promised and had never existed**; ③ `src/dream/interactive-dream.js:14` destructured `LEVELS` from `dream-loop` as well — and never used it either. The most pointed detail is what the module's own header claims: *"L1~L6 consciousness level scoring"* and *"six-level consciousness: 觉察→自省→无我→彼岸→般若→圣人"*. A reader would reasonably take `LEVELS` to be that table. It is not — the real six-level logic lives in `dagResult.level_breakdown` and the `L1`..`L6` branches below, which have nothing to do with the exported name. **A documented capability, a plausible constant name, and an export entry, all pointing at `undefined`.** And probing for a *second* defect of the same shape found one immediately: `module.exports` ended with `DreamEngine: require('./dream.js').DreamEngine` — but `dream.js` exports `DreamV11`, **not `DreamEngine`**, so that entry was `undefined` too. Two of the seventeen exported keys were phantoms. The fix deletes all three sites of both names (the destructure, the `LEVELS` export, the `DreamEngine` re-require — replaced by the already-aliased local `DreamEngine`), rather than inventing a `LEVELS` table: **removing a promise that never had a value is more honest than manufacturing the value**, and the capability the header advertises is real and untouched. `test/dream-loop-destructure-contract.test.js` (7 cases) locks it in a form that generalises past this module: every exported key must have a real value (this is the case that caught the *second* phantom, which I had not gone looking for); every name destructured from `dream.js` must actually be exported by it — checked on the **module side** of `A: B` aliases, because my first version read the local name and reported `DreamV11: DreamEngine` as missing, a check that was wrong about its own subject; `interactive-dream` must not re-destruct it; a source-level ban on both names; a reverse proof that re-adds `LEVELS` and requires the lock to go red by name; and a final case that the deletion broke no real contract. **The reverse proof is where this cycle's instrument lesson lives, and it is a new costume on an old one.** My first mutation added `LEVELS,` to `module.exports` *without* restoring the destructure — and the module then failed to load at all with `LEVELS is not defined`, because an exports list referencing an undeclared identifier is a `ReferenceError`. That is not the defect being tested: **before the fix the module loaded fine**, precisely *because* the destructure had given `LEVELS` an undefined "definition". A mutation that makes the code louder than the original defect proves nothing about the lock. Redone by restoring **both** the destructure and the export, which reproduces the original exactly: loads cleanly, `LEVELS === undefined`, `'LEVELS' in exports === true` — and the lock goes **5/7 red by name**. Then the restore step broke in a way worth recording: my restore script `replace`d the old declaration with a new one **whose explanatory comment contained the old declaration verbatim**, so the pattern matched inside the comment first and the file ended up with nested duplicated comments plus the real defect line still present — caught by `node --check` reporting `Identifier 'DreamEngine' has already been declared`. **A repair comment that quotes the code it repairs is a pattern that will match itself** (cycle 19's stripper, cycle 25's `}));`, cycle 194's un-stripped comment — fourth installment), and the fix is the same each time: strip comments before matching, or do not quote the offending code in the repair note. **Three further errors in my own lock, all caught by running it.** (i) The source-level ban pattern-matched my own comment quoting the removed code and reported the file guilty; it now blanks line comments first — and asserts it still finds at least one `require(./dream*.js)` declaration, so the check cannot become vacuous. (ii) `assertEqual` is `!==`, so `assertEqual(tokenize(...), ['the','quick'])` fails on two equal arrays with different references; rewritten to compare length and `join`. (iii) The alias parser took the **local** name from `DreamV11: DreamEngine` instead of the module-side name, so the "must be exported by dream.js" case reported a name that *is* exported under its other spelling. Gate: verify **15/15**; run-all **1897 → 1904 / 0 / 1904 with exit 0** (one clean run out of five; the other four gave 1880–1903 with 1–6 failures, every one an audit-family or doc-probe-lock file that is green alone — the cycle-194 concurrency artefact, unchanged and still the gate's largest instability source); audit **exit 0, 94 claims / 94 consistent / 0 mismatched**. The B-class anchor cascade fired for the **twelfth** time (148/153/156/159/167/170/171/179/191/193/194/195): moving `dream-loop.js` from B to C took B **98 → 97** and C **273 → 274**, and SKILL.md's two coverage readings were synced. Suite **1897 → 1904**, all seven new cases from the new lock. `src/` changed in two files, both inside a `require` destructure and an exports list — no MCP tool definition, handler mapping or permission contract was added or modified, so MCP was **not** restarted (recorded as not-performed). **One repair carried over from the previous cycle, because it was committed broken and only this cycle's gate exposed it:** cycle 194's `sed` wrote `**999 alive but referenced by no test**` into SKILL.md's Coverage sweep row while the prose half of the same line read 98 — a number nobody had ever claimed, in the one row the audit checks. It was committed that way, and the audit-family instability of that cycle meant nothing ever measured it. `coverage-sweep-doc-claims.test.js` went red on it this cycle, the row was corrected to 98 (then to 97 as the cascade moved), and the case is 4/4 green. **A tool that reports success while having written the wrong thing is worse than one that fails, and `sed` reported success.**
- **A graph search that could never find a path — and the caller whose shape assumption it had been hiding for its entire life.** `test-coverage-gap`, 196th cycle, and the engine chose the same slice as cycles 194 and 195 (0.77/0.70). The first three cycles on this slice had each opened with a different question for picking a module inside class B — *crisp contract* (191), *promised by full path in a document* (194), *exports pure functions and constant tables* (195) — and all three were now used up. This cycle asked a fourth: **which B-class module has the highest `src/` fan-in?** `coverage-sweep.js` measured 383 modules / 12 suspected dead / **96 alive-but-untested** / 275 referenced. Ranking the B list by how many other `src/` files `require` it put `src/memory/knowledge-graph.js` first with **6 consumers and zero tests**. Probing it by *calling* it found the defect on the first shape that mattered: `findPath('A','C')` returned `[]` on a graph that plainly contains A→B→C. The cause is one line. On hit it wrote `path.push({...}); return;` — **and that `return` exits the `forEach` callback, not `findPath`.** The BFS therefore walked the entire graph and fell through to the function's final `return []`. So `findPath` returned an empty array for every input, forever. And it has a real consumer: `aspira_knowledge_graph`'s `path` action calls it (`mcp-server.js:3502`), an advertised capability that could only ever say "not found" — *the call succeeds, nothing throws, the response is structurally valid, and the content is dead.* A second defect sat in the same function: on hit it set `triples: [t]`, recording **only the edge that hit** rather than every edge along the way, so even a returned path could not say which relations it traversed. Two further defects fell out of the same probe. `getStats()` reported `triplesAdded` (cumulative, including LRU-evicted entries) and three index sizes but **never the current triple count**, so the same object produced `save() → count 250`, `getStats() → triplesAdded 250`, and `query({}) → 100` (the default `limit`) with no field anywhere that lets a caller tell whether the 100 is a truncation or the whole graph. And `constructor(dataDir)` did no type check, so the sole `src/` caller's `new KnowledgeGraph({ silent: true })` made `dataDir` an **object**, which travelled silently into `path.join(this.dataDir, …)` and surfaced as `TypeError: The "path" argument must be of type string` — meaning "save without a filePath and use the default directory" was dead for the only caller, and dead in a form nobody could read. All three are fixed: `findPath` returns the complete path with all accumulated edges; `getStats()` gains `triples` (current stock, deliberately kept separate from `triplesAdded` throughput — LRU eviction is exactly what separates them); `dataDir` is normalised to `null` unless it is a non-empty string, which turns the `TypeError` into a readable domain error. A fourth defect was found, measured, and **deliberately not fixed**: `aspira_knowledge_graph`'s handler constructs a **fresh instance on every call** (`mcp-server.js:3477`), so `add` writes into an object that dies with the call and `query`/`search`/`related`/`path` always run against an empty graph. Fixing it means deciding how long an instance should live (process singleton / per-session / on disk), which is product judgement — 铁律 2's explicit exclusion — so it is pinned in the test as a disclosed limitation with instructions to invert the assertion when it is fixed. **Then the fix broke a caller, and that is the most valuable thing this cycle found.** `test/knowledge-graph-adapter.test.js` went red with `p.map is not a function`. `knowledge-graph-adapter.js:87` had been written as `paths.map(p => p.map(t => …))` — it expected `findPath` to return `Array<Array<triple>>`. But `findPath` had *always returned `[]`*, so that line had been executing against an empty array since the day it was written and **its shape assumption had never once been exercised.** Repairing the底层 turned a silent phantom into a loud `TypeError` in the layer above. The generalisation is worth stating plainly: **a function that always returns the empty result hides every shape mismatch in all of its callers** — the defect is not confined to the function, it is distributed across everyone who adapted to it, and none of them can be found by reading the function. `findDomainPath` now consumes the new node shape correctly, and the lock carries a dedicated case that runs the adapter end-to-end so the next repair cannot ship the same way. `test/knowledge-graph-contract.test.js` (10 cases) covers: path found and node sequence; all traversed edges present; the three legitimate "not found" shapes still `[]` (so the repair did not trade recall for a permissive matcher); `maxDepth` still binding; `getStats().triples` making truncation discoverable; `triples` vs `triplesAdded` separating under LRU eviction; `dataDir` normalisation in both directions; the adapter end-to-end; and the disclosed per-call-instance defect. Mutation-verified in three directions: reverting `findPath` to the push-and-return form turns **3** red by name; removing `triples` turns **2** red; removing the `dataDir` check turns **2** red — each restored to 10/10 afterwards. **Three process notes, and the first is a re-payment of cycle 194's most expensive lesson.** (a) I made the mutation, then copied the mutated file to `/tmp/kg-fixed.js` as "the fixed version" before `git checkout`ing — **a backup taken after the mutation is a backup of the defect**, and I only caught it because the next mutation run's precondition assertion refused to fire (`findPath 修复不在位`). The three fixes were rebuilt from the edit record rather than from the backup. The rule now has a mechanical guard: the mutation script asserts all three fixes are present *before* it will apply anything, so a stale tree cannot be silently mutated. (b) A `sed -i` meant to patch a `\n` inside the restore script instead **deleted the anchor text**, leaving `orig.replace('      ', '')` — a replacement that would have stripped the first six spaces of the file. It was caught by the script's own anchor assertion, not by reading it. **A one-liner that edits a script is editing code you have not read.** (c) `git checkout -- src/memory/knowledge-graph.js` between mutations wiped all three fixes at once, and the restore script then failed on its ② anchor because ①'s inserted comment had changed the surrounding text — a repair script whose anchors are order-dependent. Rebuilt by running the three replacements in a fixed order with per-step assertions. Gate: verify **15/15**; run-all **1904 → 1914 / 0 / 1914 with exit 0** (two prior runs gave 1896/18, every failure an audit-family file green alone); audit **exit 0, 95 claims / 95 consistent / 0 mismatched**. The B-class anchor cascade fired for the **thirteenth** time (148/153/156/159/167/170/171/179/191/193/194/195/196): moving `knowledge-graph.js` from B to C took B **97 → 96** and C **274 → 275**, and SKILL.md's two coverage readings were synced. Suite **1904 → 1914**, all ten new cases from the new lock. `src/` changed in two files — one function body plus one constructor line in `knowledge-graph.js`, and `findDomainPath`'s consumption of the new shape in the adapter — no MCP tool definition, handler mapping or permission contract was added or modified, so MCP was **not** restarted (recorded as not-performed). **One repair carried over from cycle 193 and found again here:** the probe `The engine ships only 131 modules in total.` was sitting in `README.md` unrestored, and the audit reported `README claims modules = 131, measured 132`. Per the rule cycle 193 recorded — *when a documented count enters a feedback loop, audit for unrestored probes before touching the count* — it was removed by anchor first, and only then did the test-count reading converge to 1914/0.
- **A rule engine that answered the same question three different ways — because its "minimal response" was picked with `Math.random()`.** `test-coverage-gap`, 197th cycle, and the engine chose the same slice as cycles 194, 195 and 196 (0.77/0.70). The first four cycles on this slice had each opened with a different question for picking a module inside class B — *crisp contract* (191), *promised by full path in a document* (194), *exports pure functions and constant tables* (195), *highest `src/` fan-in* (196) — and all four were used up. This cycle asked a fifth: **which B-class module does an MCP tool call directly — the outward promise surface — with zero tests?** `coverage-sweep.js` measured 383 modules / 12 suspected dead / **95 alive-but-untested** / 276 referenced. Fifteen of the B list are `require`d by `src/mcp-server.js`, each backing one advertised tool. `src/shield/spontaneous-restraint.js` (497 lines, `shield` family) was the one whose promise is the repo's core value stated outright: its header reads *"道法自然 — 最好的行动是不行动"*, *"当用户不需要答案时，沉默比说话更有价值"*, and its tool `aspira_spontaneous_restraint` returns `evaluate(text)` — the discriminator deciding, unprompted, to say less. Probing it by **calling** it found the defect in one line: `computeMinimalForm()` chose its answer with `forms[Math.floor(Math.random() * forms.length)]`. Measured: the same input `你好` across 20 calls produced **three different results** (我在。9 / 嗯。6 / 然后？5), and because `evaluate()` copies that into its `minimalForm` field, the randomness ships straight to the MCP caller. Three consequences, and the third is the one this repository exists to prevent: ① **not reproducible** — the same input yields a different "verdict" every call; ② **not testable** — any assertion about `minimalForm` degrades to "it is one of these three"; ③ **it contradicts what the engine is** — AGENTS.md names *discriminator-first* and *auditable, every decision preserves its full reasoning chain*, and a rule engine that answers the same question differently each time has no verdict at all, nor any way to say why. The fix replaces the random index with a content-derived one (`hash` of the input's char codes, modulo the form list), so the same input always yields the same form while different inputs still spread across the list — the second property matters as much as the first, because an implementation that always returns `forms[0]` would satisfy determinism by flattening every input onto one answer, which is a different distortion. `test/spontaneous-restraint-contract.testract.js` (10 cases) locks both directions: the same input 30 times must yield exactly one form; twelve *different* inputs must still reach at least three distinct forms (so the fix cannot be bought by constant-folding); category classification unchanged and each category may only return a form from its own table; determinism must hold all the way out to `evaluate()`'s `minimalForm` because that is the field the MCP tool returns; `aggressiveness` must still change restraint (0 → `minimal`, 0.5/1 → `full`, measured before the fix so the repair cannot silently flatten it); a source-level ban on `Math.random` inside the function; a reverse proof that reinstates it; and a final case pinning the disclosed per-call-instance defect. Mutation-verified: restoring `Math.random` turns **4** cases red by name, including the source-level ban and the reverse proof itself; restoring the deterministic form returns 10/10. **Two things measured and deliberately not changed, both recorded rather than papered over.** (a) `shouldRestrainExpansion` returns `{restrain:false}` for short inputs — its gate is `lengthGate * 1.5 = 450` characters, so this is a working length gate, not a defect; my first probe misjudged it as恒 false because I fed it short strings. (b) The handler constructs a **fresh instance per call** (`mcp-server.js:4180`), so `stats()`/`history` never accumulate across calls — the same shape cycle 196 found on `knowledge-graph`, and fixing it means deciding instance lifetime, which is product judgement and 铁律 2's explicit exclusion. **Three errors in my own lock, all caught by running it.** (i) I asserted `timestamp` was a `number`; it is an ISO **string** (`shouldIntervene` uses it as the audit trail). (ii) The source-level check located the function with `indexOf('computeMinimalForm(')` — which hit the **call site** inside `evaluate()` at line 238, not the definition, so the assertion was reading somebody else's function body: cycle 191's exact lesson, fifth installment. It now anchors on the full signature `computeMinimalForm(userMessage, context = {})`. (iii) After fixing (ii) the case went red on `Math.random` being present — because **my own repair comment quoted the removed code verbatim**, and the source-level matcher read its own documentation as the offence. That is the cycle 19/25/194/195 pattern for the fifth time, and the fix is the same each time: blank line comments before matching. **One process note, and it is the same lesson in a new costume:** my restore script re-inserted the entire explanatory comment block plus the code, so the file ended with `let hash` declared twice and `node --check` reported `Identifier 'hash' has already been declared` — the same failure cycle 195 recorded as *a repair comment that quotes the code it repairs is a pattern that will match itself*. The duplicate block was removed by anchor, and the file verified single-declaration, `Math.random`-free (comments blanked), 10/10 on the lock, and deterministic on a live call. Gate: verify **15/15**; run-all **1914 → 1924 / 0 / 1924 with exit 0** (one clean run; the immediately preceding run gave 1906/18, every failure an audit-family file green alone — the cycle-194 concurrency artefact); audit **exit 0, 96 claims / 96 consistent / 0 mismatched**. The B-class anchor cascade fired for the **fourteenth** time (148/153/156/159/167/170/171/179/191/193/194/195/196/197): moving `spontaneous-restraint.js` from B to C took B **96 → 95** and C **275 → 276**, and SKILL.md's two coverage readings were synced. Suite **1914 → 1924**, all ten new cases from the new lock. `src/` changed in one function body — the answer-selection line inside `computeMinimalForm` — no MCP tool definition, handler mapping or permission contract was added or modified, so MCP was **not** restarted (recorded as not-performed).
- **An ethics guardian whose review function crashes on the first call in any fresh deployment — because a log line has no directory to go to.** `test-coverage-gap`, 198th cycle, and the engine chose the same slice as cycles 194–197 (0.77/0.70). The first five cycles on this slice had each opened with a different question for picking a module inside class B — *crisp contract* (191), *promised by full path in a document* (194), *exports pure functions and constant tables* (195), *highest `src/` fan-in* (196), *called directly by an MCP tool* (197) — and all five were used up. This cycle asked a sixth: **which B-class module's error handling is most likely to turn a failure into a plausible-looking answer?** The proxy was mechanical — count `catch` blocks and the shape of what they swallow. `src/shield/ethics/sage-guardian.js` (426 lines, `shield/ethics`) led with **6 catches, 4 of them swallowing silently**. Probing it by **calling** it crashed on the very first shape that mattered: on a clean temp directory, `new SAGEGuardian(root)` succeeded and `reviewProposal()` immediately threw `ENOENT: no such file or directory, open '<root>/logs/sage-guardian.log'`. The cause is two lines of absent defence. `log()` was a bare `safeAppendFileSync(this.logFile, entry)`, and `logs/` is on `.gitignore` line 85 with **no `logs/` file tracked in git at all** — so on a fresh clone the directory does not exist. The constructor `mkdir`s `data/` (inside `_saveState`) but never `logs/`. And `log()` is called on **both** branches of `reviewProposal` — approve and reject — so no review escapes it. The result: **the ethics guardian's review function necessarily crashes on a fresh deployment, and it dies on a log line** — an auxiliary action killing the primary duty. The live repo never noticed because its `logs/` happens to exist. The same file already states the right principle in a comment on `_saveState()`: *"持久化失败不阻断安全审查主流程"* — and that method carries both `mkdir` and `try/catch`. `log()` carried neither. The fix supplies both, in the same shape: `log()` now ensures its directory exists first (the audit trail is this module's job — AGENTS.md principle 3, *auditable* — so it should be made to succeed, not merely have its failure swallowed) and still never throws (a log that cannot be written must not kill the safety review). A **second defect of the same family** fell out while writing the lock: `reviewProposal(null)` and `reviewProposal(undefined)` threw `Cannot read properties of null (reading 'description')`, because `checkValueAlignment` reads `proposal.description` with no entry guard. Measured, every other shape returned a proper `passed:false` — `'str'`, `42`, `{}`, `{description:''}` — so only the two nullish forms died. A safety-review entry point that crashes on `null` is the same shape as the `ENOENT`: **the primary duty killed by an undefended boundary**. It now normalises non-object input to `{}`, which flows through the existing value-alignment gate and lands on the correct verdict — *not approved* — rather than an exception. `test/sage-guardian-contract.test.js` (11 cases) locks both: on a fresh deployment with no `logs/`, neither branch may throw; the audit trail must actually land on disk (2 lines for 2 reviews, one `APPROVE` and one `REJECT`); with `logs/` read-only the review must still return its verdict **and** still increment `violationCount` (it is the raw material of the ASL level); `violationCount` must survive across instances (7 → ASL-2, 11 → ASL-3, 3 → ASL-1) because the source comment's own words are *"累积违规监控形同虚设"* if it does not; a corrupted state file must fall back to 0 without blocking startup; cooldown semantics and persistence; the constitution-protection patterns must still reject all five dangerous phrasings; boundary inputs must not throw; a source-level requirement that `log()` carry both `mkdir` and `try/catch`; and a reverse proof. Mutation-verified in two directions: reverting `log()` to the bare call turns **10** cases red by name; removing the entry normalisation turns **1** red (the boundary-input case) — each restored to 11/11. **Four errors in my own lock, all caught by running it.** (i) The corrupted-state case wrote the state file into a `data/` directory that did not exist yet — the constructor does *not* create it (`_saveState` only does, when a review runs), so the case threw `ENOENT` for a reason that was my own test's. (ii) The reverse proof's assertion compared `broken.indexOf('try {')` against `src.indexOf('try {')` — but the first `try {` in the file lives in `_loadState()`, present in both versions, so the comparison was meaningless. It now counts `try` inside `log()`'s own body. (iii) The first reverse-proof mutation replaced only the `safeAppendFileSync` line, leaving the surrounding `try/catch` intact — so it asserted a protection was removed while the protection was still there. The mutation now replaces the whole `try` block. (iv) I expected `修复一个小 bug` to be approved; it is **rejected**, because `checkValueAlignment`'s positive-indicator list contains 心流 / 用户体验 / 帮助 / 优化 / 提升 / flow / user experience / improve and **not** 修复. That is a deliberate value gate — a proposal must state its relation to flow or experience — not a defect, and my expectation was wrong about the module's contract. **One thing measured and deliberately left alone:** the constructor *writes* `CORE_VALUES.md` when the constitution file is absent. That is intentional onboarding (no constitution → write the default one), not a defect; and `loadValues()`'s fallback to the built-in `CONSTITUTION` on read failure is a sane degradation. The cause is one line of absent defence repeated sixteen times: the only guard was the **default value** `answer = ''` / `context = {}` — and a default value covers *not passing the argument*, not *passing `null`, a number, an object, or an array explicitly*. `analyze`'s own first line made it worse: `!answer || answer.trim()` lets every truthy non-string through (`!42` is `false`), so `42.trim()` runs. - **The module whose job is to say "you are wrong" to its operator could not survive one badly-typed argument — and the pipeline's catch turned that into a silent failure.** `test-coverage-gap`, 200th cycle; `AspiraDecision` abstained on a two-way tie (`test-coverage-gap` 0.77 / `doc-honest-numbers` 0.77) and broke it by `consequence_value` (confidence 0). The first seven cycles on this slice had each opened with a different question for picking a module inside class B — *crisp contract* (191), *promised by full path in a document* (194), *exports pure functions and constant tables* (195), *highest `src/` fan-in* (196), *called directly by an MCP tool* (197), *error handling most likely to disguise failure* (198), *never validates its entry parameters* (199) — and all seven were used up. This cycle asked an eighth: **which B-class module's public methods all return the same constant?** The proxy was mechanical — a function body whose last line is `return <literal>`. Of the two candidates it surfaced, `src/core/priority-guardian.js` (308 lines) carried the promise this repository exists to keep: *"当老大指令与人类进步冲突时 → 必须有路径说'老大错了'"* — a module whose entire job is refusing its operator. Probing it by **calling** it found **20+ crashes on boundary shapes**: `check(null)` → `Cannot read properties of null (reading 'userIntent')`; `check({userIntent: 42})` → `userIntent.toLowerCase is not a function`; `check({userIntent: 'x', action: 42})` → `action.toLowerCase is not a function`; `detectConflicts(null, …)`, `detectConflicts('x', null)`, `detectConflicts('x','x',null)` each throwing on `toLowerCase` / `truthValue`; `detectToolBehavior(null, …)` on `trim`; `resolveWithGuardianPriority(null, …)` on `filter`; `buildRefusalReason(null)` and `buildConditions(null)` on `map`. The cause is the same one line of absent defence repeated nine times: the only guard was the **default value** `context = {}` / `userIntent = ''`, and a default value covers *not passing the argument*, not *passing `null`, a number, an object, or an array explicitly*. **But the consequence here is heavier than cycle 199's, and this is the part worth the cycle.** `src/pipeline.js:212` wraps the call in a `catch` that pushes `{ layer: 'priority-guardian', error: e.message }` into `checked_by`. So an entry crash is **swallowed into an audit-trail line** — the guardian does not throw, does not warn, and simply does not run, while a caller reading `checked_by` cannot distinguish "the guardian approved" from "the guardian never executed". A layer whose job is to say no could not even say "I failed to say no". The fix is two module-level helpers, `_asText` and `_asObject`, applied at the entry of all nine public methods; the full **455-shape boundary matrix** now throws zero exceptions while every refusal path is preserved (`TRUTH_SUPPRESSION` → `REFUSE_WITH_GUARDIAN` with reason and alternative; `TRANSMISSION_ABANDONMENT` → refused; `TOOL_BEHAVIOR` → `CONDITIONAL_ALLOW` with conditions; no-conflict → `ALLOW`). **Then the repair introduced a regression that only the test could see, and it is a new shape worth naming.** `detectToolBehavior`'s first predicate is `act.trim() === intent.trim()`. After normalisation both are `''` for empty input, and **two empty strings are equal** — so every nullish call was judged "tool behaviour", which pushed `check(null)` from `ALLOW` to `CONDITIONAL_ALLOW`, which `pipeline.js:208` escalates from `pass` to `verify`. *Normalisation turned "empty equals empty" into a verdict.* A one-line guard (`if either is empty return false`) closes it, and the lock carries a dedicated case pinning `check(null).path === 'ALLOW'` so the regression cannot return silently. `test/priority-guardian-contract.test.js` (11 cases) locks both directions: every public method's boundary shapes must not throw; empty input must not be judged tool-behaviour; the four refusal/conditional paths must survive; a real tool-behaviour pattern must still be detected (the repair may not trade recall for permissiveness); a source-level entry-normalisation requirement; and a disclosed limitation — `detectToolBehavior('好的，不过我觉得有问题，建议这样', '执行')` returns `true` because `noJudge` inspects the **action** rather than the user's stated judgement, which is a pre-existing logic issue present on `HEAD` as well and out of scope for one minimal change.
